\begin{enumerate}
\item If the box is empty, (V), or a proposed configuration passes the test (I), it returns $0$.
\item If the box may be dropped, (D) or (P), it returns the smaller of the drop's cost and the cost of its decomposition
  by Buchstab's identity, the latter searched with the smaller of the drop's cost and the limit as its limit.
\item Otherwise it decomposes the box at the first proposed floor at which (J2) or (J3) passes, if the box has fewer
  primes than the strategy's \emph{depth}: the new prime's range $[f, h_k)$ is cut at the proposed cuts, and each cell
  is searched with the limit that the cells before it leave; the search of the range stops as soon as the costs reach
  the limit. A box of the sign $-$ that may be reversed is reversed instead if that costs less (the strategy at $43/20$
proposes this for the boxes of the sign $-$ only; the search accepts a reversal of either sign, by the remark after
Proposition~\ref{prop:reversal}), and a box that can be
  neither decomposed nor reversed is cut in two at the middle of its newest cell, if that cell is wider than the
  strategy's least width.
\end{enumerate}
A reversal is searched in the same way: its floor is the first proposed floor at which the chain of no prime passes a
test over the frozen part, and the tree over the frozen part on $((f, \nu))$ is searched with the nodes of
Definition~\ref{def:frozen-tree}, and with the limit past which the reversal's cost (\ref{eq:cR}) would not be below
that of the decomposition (the search that the kernel follows searches it with the fixed limit $64\mathcal K$, which
no tree of the plan reaches).

What the search tries is proposed by a \emph{strategy}, which needs no proof: every proposal is tested before it is
used. At $43/20$ the strategy proposes the following.
% The strategy is the planner's (tools/linnik/hint_cert.py, on flip_cert.py and fill_cert.py; the plan carries its choices). In Lean, what its proposals are made of: SieveAd.fmax3, SieveAd.bestSub, SieveAd.stepOf; the searches that try: SieveAd.solveG, SieveAd.nodeG, SieveAd.kids.
\begin{itemize}
\item For (I): as the rest, the first prime of the box, from the smallest, whose cell the region admits for a rest and
  for which a set of the other primes has sums that the region admits for the first variable with it; and that set.
\item For (J2): for each prime that the region admits as the rest, the floor $\min(l_{\min}, v - u - 1)$ allowed by its
  window $[u, v]$, with $l_{\min}$ the least lower end of the box's cells; as $\mathcal S_0$ the set of the other primes
  of largest lower sum whose upper sum stays below $v$; and a set $\mathcal S_1$ and a side $\iota$ for which
  $(\mathrm L)$ holds, if there are any; of these, the one of highest floor.
\item For (J3): the floors $f_3 = \min(l_{\min}, f_{\max})$, $\lfloor 3f_3/4 \rfloor$, $\lfloor 3\lfloor 3f_3/4 \rfloor
  /4 \rfloor$, \dots, eight of them, where $f_{\max}$ is the largest floor with $u + f < v$ in (J3); at each, as
  $\mathcal S_0$ the set of the box's primes of largest lower sum whose upper sum stays below $v$, and the first split
  $(\mathcal S_1, \iota)$ of the leaves that passes $(\mathrm L)$. The first floor with such a split is proposed before
  (J2)'s if it is higher.
\item Cuts of the new prime's range $[f, h_k)$: cells of side at most $4398046511/D$, about $0.004$, for the first four
  primes, twice that for the fifth and sixth, and at most a tenth of their lower end, up to the exponent from which the
  new prime's square makes every box of products only; then the exponent $D - 1 - h(\mathfrak b)$, below which the new
  box has no product in $(x, 2x]$ and may be dropped; and $l_1 - 1$, below which a box may be reversed.
\item Depth $6$: boxes of fewer than six primes are decomposed, so a box has at most six. Reversed: boxes of at most six
  primes, so of three or five, since they have the sign $-$.
\item Over a frozen part: chains of fewer than three primes are decomposed; the chain's primes have cells of side at
  most $6597069767/D$, about $0.006$, twice that for the third, and at most a tenth of their lower end; and the
  configurations are those of the box with the cofactor $m$ as one more cell, whose range is read off the slab, widened
  by the room $\gamma$ for the tests at a floor.
\end{itemize}
The cells are the strategy's: any cells pass Definition~\ref{def:tree}, and the side $0.004$ and the depth $6$ affect
only the cost.

\subsection{Checking a search}

Lean's kernel can decide a proposition about natural numbers, lists and Booleans by evaluating it, trusting no code
compiled from the definitions; it computes on numerals with the GMP library (\S S5). The search is such a function: its
tests are the conditions of Definitions~\ref{def:tests},
\ref{def:frozen-tree} and~\ref{def:reversal} in integers, and its costs are the integers (\ref{eq:cD}), $c'_D$ and
(\ref{eq:cR}), every division rounded up, with $\Omega$ computed from its table. Its soundness is proved once, for every
strategy.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:search}
% Lean: SieveAd.solveG_sound, SieveAd.kidsP_sound, SieveAd.Plain.solveD_sound, SieveAd.Plain.stepD_sound, SieveAd.Plain.kidsC_sound, SieveAd.Plain.partD_sound, SieveAd.Plain.solveMD_sound, SieveAd.Plain.altFD_sound, SieveAd.Plain.pr_of_partD
Whatever the strategy, or the hints that lead the search (below):
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] if the search returns on a box, a sign and a limit a value $c$ below the limit, then the term of the box
  with that sign is bounded at $c/\mathcal K$;
\item[(b)] let $\mathfrak b$ be a box of $k \ge 0$ primes, $\varepsilon$ a sign and $\alpha_0 < \alpha_1 < \dots <
  \alpha_N$ integers. If the values of the search on the boxes $\mathfrak b \oplus (\alpha_{i - 1}, \alpha_i)$,
  $1 \le i \le N$, with the sign $\varepsilon$, the hints read in turn and each with the limit $\mathcal K$ less
  the values before it, are each below its limit and add up to at most $c < \mathcal K$, then the term of $\mathfrak b
  \oplus (\alpha_0, \alpha_N)$ with the sign $\varepsilon$ is bounded at $c/\mathcal K$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) By induction on the depth of recursion, with the cases of the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:tree-bound}. A value
below the limit comes from a node whose condition the search has evaluated to true: an empty or evaluated box, at the
cost $0$; a dropped box, at its cost; a decomposition at a floor
whose test has passed, whose cells' values, each below its limit, are the costs of trees by the induction, and add up
by cuts (S), since any point strictly inside a range is a cut; a reversal whose test at its floor has passed and whose
tree over the frozen part has, by the same induction, the cost from which (\ref{eq:cR}) is computed, or, for a box of
the sign $+$, which the search led by hints also accepts, the cost of Section~\ref{sec:reversal-plus}, by the bound
stated after Proposition~\ref{prop:reversal}; or a box cut in two. A branch whose condition fails returns its limit,
which says nothing. (b) follows from (a) by induction on $N$: each value is below its limit, so (a) bounds each
cell's term, and Lemma~\ref{lem:box-identities}(a) adds the bounds.
\end{proof}

When no box of the sign $+$ is reversed, as at $43/20$, the same induction shows that the nodes that the search took
form a tree on the box with that sign that passes, of cost at most $c$; the formalisation proves the bound alone
(\S S5).

So the certificate is a set of propositions about integers: that the search's values on the parts, ranges of cells of
the first, second or third prime with the limit $\mathcal K$, are at most their bounds; that the steps of Buchstab's
identity that join the parts pass their tests; and that the bounds add up to at most $c$. The kernel proves them by
evaluation, part by part, and the formalisation proves the bound of the term that Lemma~\ref{lem:search} and
Lemma~\ref{lem:tests}(b) draw from them. It proves the bound, rule by rule, and never writes the tree down.

\subsection{Hints}

A search that tries what its strategy proposes spends most of its time on what it does not keep: trees under boxes
that are then dropped or reversed, the test of every proposed floor where one is used, and the search for a
configuration among the sets of a box's cells. So the search is run once outside the kernel, in exact integer
arithmetic and definition by definition, and the plan it keeps is written as \emph{hints}: for each part of the check,
a natural number, read from its low end in the order in which the search visits the boxes. At a box the hints give a
run of ones closed by a zero, which names the node, and then what the node needs.
% Lean: SieveAd.solveD, SieveAd.stepD, SieveAd.cfgIP, SieveAd.cfgJP, SieveAd.cfgIM, SieveAd.cfgJM, SieveAd.cfgIF, SieveAd.cfgJF, SieveAd.floorP, SieveAd.kidsCS, SieveAd.kidsC, SieveAd.kidsM, SieveAd.kidsF, SieveAd.partD, SieveAd.Plain.altFD, SieveAd.Plain.flipCostD, SieveAd.Plain.flipTreeD, SieveAd.solve21500
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lp{0.68\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Hints & What the search does at the box \\
\midrule
\texttt{0} & evaluates the box by (I), with the configuration that follows \\
\texttt{10} & Buchstab's identity, at the floor and with the configuration that follow, then the cuts of the new
  prime's range \\
\texttt{110} & drops the box \\
\texttt{1110} & reverses the box: the floor and the configuration of the test over the frozen part, then the cuts of
  the range of the chain's first prime \\
\texttt{11110} & cuts the newest cell in two at its middle \\
\texttt{111110} & finds the box empty, (V) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
A configuration of (I) is the rest's cell, $0$ for the first or $1$ and three bits for its index, and the set $\mathcal
S$, a bit for each cell of the box. At a floor, four bits name the comparison and with it the floor: with $2j$, (J2)
with the rest the prime at position $j$, counted from the smallest from $0$, at the floor $\min(l_{\min}, v - u - 1)$ of
its window $[u, v]$; with $2i + 1$, (J3) at the floor after $i$ of its eight steps. Then come $\mathcal S_0$, a bit for
each cell, and either $0$, for the side $\iota = 1$ and the set $\mathcal S_1$ of all the cells but the last one,
which is the largest prime's, $p_1$ (over a frozen part, the cofactor's), or $1$, the set $\mathcal S_1$ and a bit for
the side. Over a frozen part every set has one more bit, the cofactor's, and the rest
of (J2) may be the cofactor. Each cut of a new prime's range is a code: one to seven steps of the strategy's grid after
the last point used, seven steps with no cut, one of the strategy's three cuts off the grid, or no further cut. The
plan joins consecutive cells that one test evaluates, and consecutive dropped cells while the joined cell's cost stays
within $0.2\%$ of the sum of theirs; so the cells of the certificate are unions of the strategy's, and may be wider than
$0.004$. The kernel searches for nothing: it reads each configuration, computes each floor from its four bits and
the box and each cut from its code, and evaluates the tests and the costs.

Lemma~\ref{lem:search} holds whatever the hints: each branch of the search led by hints is one of the nodes among which
the search chooses, with whatever configuration, floor and cut the hints give, applied only after its condition has
passed, and a cut at any point is a node (S). A hint that names a node whose condition fails makes the value the
limit, which says nothing. So the hints are not trusted: a wrong hint can make the check fail, not make it wrong.

\subsection{The certificate at \texorpdfstring{$43/20$}{43/20}}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:certificate}
% Lean: Linnik21500.tree21500 (the bound of the first term), Linnik21500.linnik_21500 (the root's test, by evaluation), Linnik21500.part21500, Linnik21500.sound21500, Linnik21500.soundF21500, SieveAd.Plain.fillTests_sound, SieveAd.Plain.fillTestsF_eq, SieveAd.Plain.frozTestsC_okI, SieveAd.Plain.frozTestsC_okJ, SieveAd.Plain.rules_frozC, SieveAd.pr_buch, SieveAd.pr_split, SieveAd.pr_mono
Let $L = 43/20$ and $f_* = 49170839016$. The box of no prime passes (J3) at $f_*$ with $\mathcal S_0 = \mathcal S_1 =
\emptyset$ and $\iota = 1$, and the term of the box $((f_*, D/2 + 1))$ with the sign $-$ is bounded at $c/\mathcal K$,
by the search of this section on its parts and the steps of Buchstab's identity that join them, with
\[
  c = 1093510193343867034 = 0.94846890\ldots\, \mathcal K < 0.94847\, \mathcal K.
\]
\end{proposition}

The proposition is a computation, which the kernel carries out (Section~\ref{sec:lean}); with Theorem~\ref{thm:tree} it
gives Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} (Section~\ref{sec:proof}). At the root, $f_*/D = 0.0447206\ldots$, $r_1 = 255700380$,
and the window of (J3) is $[u, v]$ with $u = 511328600504$ and $v = 560499439521$, that is $[0.4650507, 0.5097713]$ in
exponents of $x$: $f_* = v - u - 1$ is the largest floor with $u + f_* < v$, and $(\mathrm L)$ reads $(u + r_2)/D =
0.5100038\ldots < 1 - 1/L = 0.5348837\ldots$. The window is wider than the floor only below about $(L - 2)/((2 -
\lambda)L) = 0.0448$: a rest of exponent $\rho$ moves the window's lower end down by $\lambda\rho$ and its upper end
down by $(1 - \lambda)\rho$, and the rest of (J3) grows with the floor.

The check is in parts. The first prime's range $[f_*, D/2 + 1)$ is cut into $104$ pieces: three parts, ranges of its
cells searched with the limit $\mathcal K$ and led by hints, cover $[f_*, 0.1007)$, and $101$ cells, $99$ of side
$4398046511/D$ from $110743490170/D = 0.10072\ldots$ and two up to $D/2 + 1$, have their own step of Buchstab's identity
taken outside the parts and the next prime's range cut again into parts and further cells. In all $256$ cells are cut
so, $101$ of the first prime and $155$ of the second; $227$ of their steps of Buchstab's identity use (J3), $29$ use
(J2), and $217$ are at the floor $f_*$. There are $1552$ parts, in $17$ files. Lemma~\ref{lem:box-identities}(a) and~(b)
join the pieces, and their costs add up to $c$ exactly. The pieces' costs show where the cost goes:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
\toprule
$\log p_1/\log x$ & cost$/\mathcal K$ \\
\midrule
$[0.0447, 0.1007)$ & $0.0009$ \\
$[0.1007, 0.2007)$ & $0.0154$ \\
$[0.2007, 0.3007)$ & $0.1383$ \\
$[0.3007, 0.4007)$ & $0.4042$ \\
$[0.4007, 0.5]$ & $0.3897$ \\
\midrule
all & $0.9485$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
More than four fifths of the cost is in the cells of the first prime above $x^{0.3}$, and the most expensive cells of
side
$0.004$ cost about $0.02\mathcal K$ each, near $p_1 = x^{0.41}$ and $x^{0.49}$; below $x^{0.2}$ the first prime costs
less than $0.02\mathcal K$ in all.

The kernel's search takes $3400745$ steps: $3400489$ in the parts and the $256$ steps of Buchstab's identity outside
them; $161674$ of them are reversals through the largest prime. The hints take $5220964$ bytes. The search outside the
kernel that made the plan visits $34356403$ boxes, $28257594$ of them over frozen parts, in about nine minutes on four
cores. The kernel checks the $17$ files in about $63$ minutes, each in $143$ to $271$ seconds;
\S S5 and \S S6 have the statistics in more detail.

\section{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320}}\label{sec:proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320}]
Take $L = 43/20$. Theorem~\ref{thm:type2}, under Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}, is the Type~II estimate in two variables
on the region $\mathcal R_L$, so the comparisons of Section~\ref{sec:type1} hold at $L$, and with them the tests of
Section~\ref{sec:decomp}. Proposition~\ref{prop:certificate} gives a floor $f_*$ at which the box of no prime passes
(J3), and bounds the term of the root box with the sign $-$ at $c/\mathcal K$, with $c = 1093510193343867034 <
\mathcal K = 2^{60}$. Theorem~\ref{thm:tree} gives Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} under the input.
Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} enters through Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-sum} and the top bin in Section~\ref{sec:type2},
through the kernel bound of Section~\ref{sec:two-bounds} (where the Pólya--Vinogradov inequality would do instead),
through the piece of $g_{\mathrm H}$ above $11/12$, which only makes $g_{\mathrm H}$ a density curve on all of
$[1/2, 1]$, and, in the formalisation, through the prime number theorem, which it derives from the case of $\zeta$
in the form $\psi(t) - t = O(t^{11/12 + \varepsilon})$ and uses for the main term of Theorem~\ref{thm:tree} and for the
sums over primes of Sections~\ref{sec:type1} and~\ref{sec:decomp}.
\end{proof}

The proof does not compute $K$: the threshold $q_0$ comes from limits in $q$ in each step, and the small moduli
from Dirichlet's theorem. The argument needs no step on an exceptional zero, since Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} leaves
none; the paper does not claim that its constants are effective, and its thresholds are in any case
enormous: the cells of exponents are multiples of $2^{-40}$, and the steps of Section~\ref{sec:decomp} ask, among
other things, $x^{2^{-40}} \ge 3$, that is $q \ge 3^{2^{40}/L}$.

\section{The resonant window}\label{sec:window}

Sections~\ref{sec:type2} to~\ref{sec:proof} bound the prime sums of a rest only through the zeros. In the bin of
$\beta$ each prime sum of length $Q$ is at most about $Q^\beta$, and the bin has at most about $q^{g(\beta)}$
characters, whatever the rest. In two variables this suffices on the region $\mathcal R_L$, where the number of
characters meets only the kernel's bound. In one variable it does not: Cauchy--Schwarz is taken over the bin, the
bin's size enters orthogonality as $q^{g(\beta)/2}$, and for a polynomial of length about $q$ the estimate needs $L \ge
1 + A/2$ for the largest density exponent $A$ of the curve (the remark at the end of Section~\ref{sec:type2}): $11/5$
with Huxley's $12/5$ and $13/6$ with Chen, Gupta and Li's $7/3$, both above $21/10$. So a term of the sieve whose rest
is counted only through the zeros' bins is evaluated in one variable only where the largest density exponent allows it.

The estimate does not need the number of characters that have a zero. It needs the number of characters at which the
rest's own prime sums are large, and those are polynomials of known lengths. The $k$-th power of a prime sum of length
$Q = q^s$ is a Dirichlet polynomial of length about $Q^k$ whose coefficients are bounded in terms of $k$; its mean
value over the characters and Input~\ref{inp:cgl} count the characters at which it is large. The sieves for primes in
short intervals count their own polynomials in the same way \cite{hb-iwaniec, bhp, harman}.

A heuristic estimate shows what the counts give. It is a remark, not a theorem of the library, and the paper does not
use it. Let $W$ have length $P \ge q$ and coefficients of modulus at most $1$, let the rest have the prime sums
$S_{Q_j}$, $Q_j = q^{s_j}$, with $P Q_1 \cdots Q_K = q^L$, and let $k_j$ be the least integer with $k_j s_j \ge 1$. If
$\sum_j 1/k_j \ge 1$, take $a_j \in [0, 1]$ with $\sum_j a_j/k_j = 1$, and write $|S_{Q_j}| \le |S_{Q_j}|^{a_j} U_j^{1 -
a_j}$, where $U_j \approx Q_j^{11/12 + \varepsilon}$, for a small $\varepsilon > 0$, is the bound that
Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} gives through Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-sum}. By H\"older's inequality with the exponents $2$ and
$2k_j/a_j$, the sum over $\chi \ne \chi_0$ of $|W(\chi)| \prod_j |S_{Q_j}(\chi)|$ is at most $(\sum_\chi |W|^2)^{1/2}
\prod_j (\sum_\chi |S_{Q_j}|^{2k_j})^{a_j/(2k_j)} U_j^{1 - a_j}$. Since $Q_j^{k_j} \ge q$, orthogonality gives
$\sum_\chi |S_{Q_j}|^{2k_j} \ll Q_j^{2k_j}$, and $\sum_\chi |W|^2 \ll P^2$; so the sum is $\ll q^L \prod_j Q_j^{-(1/12 -
\varepsilon)(1 - a_j)}$, and $\sum_j s_j(1 - a_j) \ge \sum_j (1 - a_j)/k_j = \sum_j 1/k_j - 1$. So when $\sum_j 1/k_j >
1$ the sum is $\ll q^{L - \kappa}$ with $\kappa = (1/12 - \varepsilon)(\sum_j 1/k_j - 1)$. A rest of one prime never
passes this test, nor a rest of two primes both below $q$; two primes above $q^{1/2}$ and any third prime pass, and so
do primes above $q^{1/2}$, $q^{1/3}$ and $q^{1/5}$.

This section proves an estimate of that kind and gives its tests. Section~\ref{sec:window-estimate} counts the
characters at which a prime sum is large, sorts the characters of a bin by the sizes of the rest's prime sums, and
proves the estimate in one variable (Theorem~\ref{thm:window}) under a condition on the exponents of every class.
Section~\ref{sec:window-terms} carries it to the sieve's terms, Section~\ref{sec:corners} ties the variable to the
rest on a box, and Section~\ref{sec:window-tests} gives the tests that the certificate's kernel checks.

\subsection{The estimate}\label{sec:window-estimate}

Throughout, $q > 1$, $S_Q(\chi, h)$ is the smoothed prime sum of Section~\ref{sec:type2}, and
\[
  W(\chi) = \sum_{n \in S} a_n \chi(n) n^{-ih_0}, \qquad S \subseteq (P, 2P], \qquad V = \sum_{n \in S} |a_n|^2,
\]
is \emph{the variable}, of length $P = q^m$. The \emph{rest} is a product of prime sums $S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)$, $1 \le j
\le K$, with $Q_j = q^{s_j}$. Exponents are exponents of $q$, except the sizes $t_j$ below, which are exponents of
$Q_j$.

\subsubsection*{The characters at which a prime sum is large}

For an integer $k \ge 1$ and reals $s$ and $u$ let
\[
  \mathfrak m_k(s, u) = \max(1, ks) + ks(1 - 2u).
\]

\begin{lemma}[the moments]\label{lem:moments}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.card_Sp_large, SieveTypeIIHM.sum_chars_Sp_pow_le, SieveTypeIIHM.sum_chars_dp_pow_le, SieveTypeIIHM.card_large_le, SieveTypeIIHM.card_tuples_primes_le, SieveTypeIIHM.momExp
Let $q > 1$, let $F_0$ bound $|f_0|$, let $Q \ge 1$ (the prime sum $S_Q$ is defined by the same formula for every $Q
\ge 1$), let $h, u \in \mathbb R$ and let $k \ge 1$ be an integer. With $s = \log_q Q$,
\[
  \#\{\chi \bmod q : |S_Q(\chi, h)| \ge Q^u\} \le (2^k + 1)\, k^k (3F_0^2)^k\, q^{\mathfrak m_k(s, u)}.
\]
\end{lemma}

The count is over all the characters mod $q$, the trivial one included. The proof bounds the $2k$-th moment of the
prime sum by orthogonality on its $k$-th power and applies Markov's inequality.

\begin{proof}
The prime sum is a Dirichlet polynomial $\sum_{p \le 2Q} c_p \chi(p)$ over the primes up to $2Q$, with $c_p =
(p/Q)^{-ih} f_0(p/Q)$, so $|c_p| \le F_0$ and $\sum_p |c_p|^2 \le F_0^2(2Q + 1) \le 3F_0^2 Q$. Its $k$-th power is
$\sum_n c^{(k)}_n \chi(n)$, where $c^{(k)}_n$ is the sum of $c_{p_1} \cdots c_{p_k}$ over the $k$-tuples of primes $p_i
\le 2Q$ with $p_1 \cdots p_k = n$, and $n \le (2Q)^k$. A tuple with the product $n$ has its entries among the prime
factors of any one such tuple, of which there are at most $k$; so there are at most $k^k$ such tuples, and by
Cauchy--Schwarz $\sum_n |c^{(k)}_n|^2 \le k^k (\sum_p |c_p|^2)^k$. Orthogonality on the interval $(0, (2Q)^k]$, as in
Section~\ref{sec:two-bounds} (each residue class mod $q$ has at most $(2Q)^k/q + 1$ elements in it), gives
\[
  \sum_{\chi \bmod q} |S_Q(\chi, h)|^{2k} \le \bigl((2Q)^k + q\bigr)\, k^k (3F_0^2 Q)^k.
\]
The characters with $|S_Q(\chi, h)| \ge Q^u$ contribute at least $Q^{2ku}$ each to the left side. With $Q = q^s$, $s
\ge 0$, and $(2Q)^k + q \le (2^k + 1) q^{\max(1, ks)}$, their number is at most $(2^k + 1) k^k (3F_0^2)^k$ times $q$ to
the power $\max(1, ks) + ks - 2ksu = \mathfrak m_k(s, u)$.
\end{proof}

For $ks \ge 1$ the exponent is $2ks(1 - u)$: where $Q^k$ is just above $q$, the count that a density exponent $2$ would
give. The second count is by Input~\ref{inp:cgl}, applied to the $k$-th power. Let $\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}} = 10^{-5}$
and
\[
  \mathfrak r_k(s, u) = \max\Bigl(2ks(1 - u),\ \frac12 + ks(3 - 4u),\ \frac43 + ks(2 - 4u)\Bigr),
\]
the exponents of the three terms of Input~\ref{inp:cgl} for a sum of length $q^{ks}$ at the size $q^{ksu}$, at $T = 1$.
Let $\mathfrak v_k(s, u) = 1$, except where $ks \ge 2/3 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$ and $u \ge 7/10 +
\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$; there
\[
  \mathfrak v_k(s, u) = \min\Bigl(1,\ \mathfrak r_k\bigl(s, \min(u, 4/5 - \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}})\bigr) +
  \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}
  \Bigr).
\]
So $0 \le \mathfrak v_k(s, u) \le 1$ for $s \ge 0$, $\mathfrak v_k(s, u)$ does not increase in $u$, and it is $1$ for
$u < 7/10 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$ and for $ks \ge 5/2$. At $u = 3/4$ and $5/6
\le ks \le 1$ it is $1/2 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$, where the moment gives $1 - ks/2$.

\begin{lemma}[the large values of the powers]\label{lem:lv-powers}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.card_Sp_large_lv, SieveTypeIIHM.card_Sp_pow_large, SieveTypeIIHM.lvExp, SieveTypeIIHM.lvRaw, LinnikCGLLV.cgl_all, LinnikCGLLV.lv_range
Assume Input~\ref{inp:cgl}. Let $k_{\max} \ge 1$ be an integer and $F_1 \ge 1$. There are $C \ge 1$ and $q_0$ such that
for every $q \ge q_0$, every real function $f$ with $|f| \le F_1$ and support in $[1/2, 2]$, all reals $Q \ge 4$,
$h$ and $u$, and every integer $1 \le k \le k_{\max}$, the prime sum $S_Q(\chi, h)$ formed with $f$ in place of $f_0$
satisfies
\[
  \#\{\chi \bmod q : |S_Q(\chi, h)| \ge Q^u\} \le C\, q^{\mathfrak v_k(\log_q Q,\, u)}.
\]
\end{lemma}

The proof applies Input~\ref{inp:cgl} at $T = 1$, at the point $0$ for every character, to the dyadic blocks of the
$k$-th power, and then compares exponents.

\begin{proof}
\emph{All characters.} The proof uses Input~\ref{inp:cgl} in the form for all the characters mod $q$ stated after it
(\lean{LinnikCGLLV.\allowbreak cgl\_all}), at $T = 1$.

\emph{The power.} As in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:moments}, $S_Q(\chi, h)^k = \sum_n c^{(k)}_n \chi(n)$ with
$|c^{(k)}_n| \le k^k F_1^k$, and $c^{(k)}_n = 0$ outside $[(Q/2)^k, (2Q)^k]$ since $f$ vanishes outside $[1/2, 2]$.
With $N_0 = \lceil (Q/2)^k \rceil - 1$, the $2k + 1$ dyadic blocks $(N_02^i, N_02^{i+1}]$, $0 \le i \le 2k$, cover that
interval. If $|S_Q(\chi, h)|^k \ge Z$, one block, with the coefficients divided by $k^k F_1^k$, is at least $Z/B_k$ at
$\chi$, $B_k = (2k + 1) k^k F_1^k$. The count for all characters at $T = 1$ and $t_\chi = 0$, for each block,
applies when $(Q/2)^k \ge 2q^{2/3} + 2$ and $((2Q)^k)^{7/10} B_k \le Z \le ((Q/2)^k/2)^{4/5}$, and bounds the
characters with $|S_Q(\chi, h)|^k \ge Z$ by $(2k + 1) C q^\varepsilon (q(2Q)^k)^\varepsilon$ times its sum of three
terms at the length $(2Q)^k$ and the size $Z/B_k$ (Lean \lean{SieveTypeIIHM.\allowbreak card\_Sp\_pow\_large}).

\emph{The exponents.} Let $s = \log_q Q$. If $ks < 2/3 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$, or $u < 7/10 +
\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$, or $\mathfrak r_k(s, u') + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}} \ge 1$ with $u' = \min(u, 4/5 -
\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}})$, the count $\varphi(q) \le q$ suffices. Otherwise $2ks(1 - u') < 1$ gives $ks \le 5/2$, and
the previous step applies at $Z = q^{ksu'} \le |S_Q(\chi, h)|^k$ with $\varepsilon = \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}/5$: the
margins $ks(u' - 7/10) \ge \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}/2$ and $ks(4/5 - u') \ge \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}/2$ absorb the powers
of $2$ and $B_k \le B_{k_{\max}}$ once $q^{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}/2} \ge 4 \cdot 2^{k_{\max}} B_{k_{\max}}$, and
$q^\varepsilon (q(2Q)^k)^\varepsilon \le 2^k q^{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}}$. Each of the three terms is
then a constant times $q$ to a term of $\mathfrak r_k(s, u')$, so the count is at most a constant, depending on
$k_{\max}$ and $F_1$ only, times $q^{\mathfrak r_k(s, u') + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}}$.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection*{The classes}

Let $\tau = 10^{-6}$. The \emph{level} of the $j$-th prime sum at $\chi$ is
\[
  \ell_j(\chi) = \min\bigl(10^6,\ \lfloor \tau^{-1} \log_{Q_j} |S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \rfloor\bigr),
\]
the integer part taken as $0$ where the logarithm is negative and where the sum vanishes. If $\ell_j(\chi) \ge 1$ then
$|S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \ge Q_j^{\tau\ell_j(\chi)}$, and if $\ell_j(\chi) < 10^6$ then $|S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le
Q_j^{\tau(\ell_j(\chi) + 1)}$. The \emph{class} of $\chi \in \mathcal X^*$ is its bin $i$ (Section~\ref{sec:bins})
together with its levels, $(i, \ell_1(\chi), \dots, \ell_K(\chi))$; there are at most $I (10^6 + 1)^K$ classes. On a
class each prime sum is at most $2Q_j^{t_j}$, with the \emph{size} $t_j$ the smaller of the zeros' bound and the
level's, and the class has at most about $q^\gamma$ characters, with the \emph{count exponent} $\gamma$ the least of
the density curve's exponent at the bin and the exponents of Lemmas~\ref{lem:moments} and~\ref{lem:lv-powers} at the
levels. A class is bounded as a bin is in Section~\ref{sec:zone}, by orthogonality or by the Hal\'asz--Montgomery
inequality, but in one variable: Cauchy--Schwarz is taken over the class, and the inequality is used in its first-moment
form, $(\sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X} |W(\chi)|)^2 \le eV(2P|\mathcal X| + |\mathcal X|^2 \mathcal H)$, which follows from
Lemma~\ref{lem:hm-sq} (the remark after it). With $\mathcal X$ of about $q^\gamma$ characters, the rest at most about
$q^E$ on it, and $V^{1/2} = q^{m/2}(V/P)^{1/2}$, the three bounds have the exponents of $q$ below.

\begin{definition}\label{def:fit}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.ResBinOK
Let $L$, $E$, $\gamma$ and $m$ be real, and let $b(m) = 5/12 + m/12 + \delta'(1 - m) + 2\varepsilon'$, $\delta' =
\varepsilon' = 1/1000$, be the exponent of the kernel bound (Section~\ref{sec:two-bounds}). The exponents $(E,
\gamma, m)$ \emph{fit} at $L$ if
\[
  E + \frac\gamma2 + \frac{m + \max(m, 1)}2 \le L - \frac1{25000},
\]
or both
\[
  E + \frac\gamma2 + m \le L - \frac1{25000} \qquad \text{and} \qquad E + \frac m2 + \gamma + \frac{b(m)}2 \le L -
  \frac1{25000}.
\]
\end{definition}

The first is orthogonality, $|\mathcal X|^{1/2}((P + q)V)^{1/2}$; the other two are the diagonal term $2P|\mathcal X|$
and the off-diagonal term $|\mathcal X|^2 \mathcal H$ of the Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality, each of which must pass.
For $m \ge 1$ the first of the two is the bound by orthogonality, so the inequality helps only for a variable shorter
than $q$. The condition on the exponents asks this of every class that the counts allow.

\begin{definition}\label{def:res}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.ResCond, SieveCurveHx.ghx
Let $L$ and $m$ be real, $K \ge 0$ an integer, $s_1, \dots, s_K$ real, and $\tau = 10^{-6}$. An \emph{admissible tuple}
at $(s_1, \dots, s_K)$ is a tuple $(\beta, \gamma, t_1, \dots, t_K)$ of reals with $1/2 \le \beta \le 11/12$, $\gamma
\le
g_{\mathrm H}(\beta)$ and, for $1 \le j \le K$,
\begin{gather*}
  t_j \le \beta + 4 \cdot 10^{-6}, \\
  \gamma \le \mathfrak m_k(s_j, t_j - \tau) \quad \text{and} \quad \gamma \le \mathfrak v_k(s_j, t_j - \tau) \qquad
  \text{for every integer } k \ge 1.
\end{gather*}
The \emph{resonance condition} $\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1, \dots, s_K)$ holds if for every admissible tuple at $(s_1,
\dots, s_K)$ the exponents
\[
  \Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^K s_j(t_j - 1) + L - m,\ \gamma,\ m\Bigr)
\]
fit at $L$.
\end{definition}

The first exponent is that of $\prod_j Q_j^{t_j} = q^{\sum_j s_j(t_j - 1)} \prod_j Q_j$ when $\prod_j Q_j \le q^{L -
m}$. The bound $t_j \le \beta + 4 \cdot 10^{-6}$ is the zeros' bound in the bin of $\beta$, rounded up: there $t_j \le
\beta_{i+1} + 2\delta = \beta + \varpi$, and $\varpi < 2.9 \cdot 10^{-6}$ (Section~\ref{sec:bins}) is rounded up
to $4 \cdot 10^{-6}$. The counts are taken one level lower, at $t_j - \tau$. The left sides of
Definition~\ref{def:fit} increase with $\gamma$ and with each $t_j$, while the hypotheses bound $\gamma$ by functions
of $t_j$ that do not increase: the condition weighs the size of a prime sum against the number of characters at which
it can be that large, and the classes that bind combine a large size with as many characters as the counts allow at
it. The condition reads $m$ and each $s_j$, not only their sum, and the variable may be shorter than $q$.

\begin{lemma}[one class]\label{lem:class}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.bin_bound_exp, SieveTypeIIHM.sum_typeII_cls, SieveTypeIIHM.sum_bin_hm, SieveTypeII.sum_chars_dp_sq_le
Let $L$, $E$ and $m$ be real, $\gamma \le 1$, and let $(E, \gamma, m)$ fit at $L$. Let $C_0, C_d, C_K \ge 0$ and
\[
  C' = e^{1/2}\bigl(2\sqrt3\, C_0 C_d^{1/2} + 6 C_0 C_d C_K^{1/2}\bigr).
\]
Let $q > 1$, $P = q^m$ an integer, $1 \le T \le 3q^\eta$ with $\eta \ge 0$, and let $0 \le \mathcal H \le C_K (2P)^{1/12
-
\delta'} (2q)^{5/12 + \delta' + 2\varepsilon'}$ bound $|k_{2P}(\psi)|$ for every non-trivial character $\psi$ mod $q$.
Let $\mathcal X$ be a set of at most $C_d (qT)^\gamma (1 + \log qT)^{10}$ characters on which $\prod_{j=1}^K
|S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le C_0 q^E$, for some $K$, $Q_j$ and $h_j$. Then for all $S \subseteq (P, 2P]$, coefficients and
$h_0$,
\[
  \sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X} |W(\chi)| \prod_{j=1}^K |S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le C' (1 + \log qT)^{10} q^{L - 1/25000 +
  \eta} \Bigl(\frac VP\Bigr)^{1/2}.
\]
\end{lemma}

The proof is that of Lemma~\ref{lem:bin}, with one polynomial: the count of the class enters both bounds.

\begin{proof}
The left side is at most $C_0 q^E \sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X} |W(\chi)|$. By Cauchy--Schwarz over $\mathcal X$ and
orthogonality over all the characters, $\sum_{\mathcal X} |W| \le |\mathcal X|^{1/2}((P + q)V)^{1/2}$; by the
first-moment form of Lemma~\ref{lem:hm-sq}, $\sum_{\mathcal X} |W| \le (eV(2P|\mathcal X| + |\mathcal X|^2
\mathcal H))^{1/2}$. Since $\gamma \le 1$, $1 \le T \le 3q^\eta$ give $(qT)^{\gamma/2} \le \sqrt3\, q^{\gamma/2 +
\eta/2}$ and $(qT)^\gamma \le 3 q^{\gamma + \eta}$, whatever the sign of $\gamma$.

If $(E, \gamma, m)$ fit by orthogonality, use the first bound: $(P + q)P \le 2q^{m + \max(m, 1)}$, so the left side is
at most $\sqrt6\, C_0 C_d^{1/2} (1 + \log qT)^5 q^{E + \gamma/2 + (m + \max(m, 1))/2 + \eta/2} (V/P)^{1/2}$, and the
exponent is at most $L - 1/25000 + \eta/2$.

Otherwise use the second:
\[
  \bigl(eV(2P|\mathcal X| + |\mathcal X|^2\mathcal H)\bigr)^{1/2} \le e^{1/2}\Bigl(\frac VP\Bigr)^{1/2}\bigl(\sqrt2\,
  |\mathcal X|^{1/2} P + P^{1/2} |\mathcal X| \mathcal H^{1/2}\bigr).
\]
The first term gives at most $\sqrt6\, e^{1/2} C_0 C_d^{1/2} (1 + \log qT)^5 (V/P)^{1/2}$ times $q$ to the power $E +
\gamma/2 + m + \eta/2$. For the second, $2^{1/12 - \delta'} 2^{5/12 + \delta' + 2\varepsilon'} \le 4$, so $\mathcal H
\le 4C_K q^{b(m)}$, and it gives at most $6 e^{1/2} C_0 C_d C_K^{1/2} (1 + \log qT)^{10} (V/P)^{1/2}$ times $q$ to the
power $E + m/2 + \gamma + b(m)/2 + \eta$. The two inequalities of Definition~\ref{def:fit} bound the exponents by $L -
1/25000 + \eta/2$ and $L - 1/25000 + \eta$. In both cases $(1 + \log qT)^5 \le (1 + \log qT)^{10}$ and $q^{\eta/2} \le
q^\eta$, and the constants are at most $C'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[the resonant window]\label{thm:window}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.typeII_res, SieveCurveHx.counts_hx, SieveCurveHx.ghx_le_one, SieveCurveHx.ghx_half
Assume Inputs~\ref{inp:half-plane} and~\ref{inp:cgl}. Let $L$ be real, let $K_0 \ge 0$ be an integer, $e_1 > 0$ and $0
< \eta \le 10^{-5}$. There is $q_0$, depending on $f_0$, $K_0$, $e_1$, $\eta$ and $L$, such that for every $q \ge q_0$,
every integer $P \ge 1$, every $S \subseteq (P, 2P]$, all coefficients $a_n$ and $h_0 \in \mathbb R$, every $K \le
K_0$, and all $Q_1, \dots, Q_K \ge q^{e_1}$ and $|h_1|, \dots, |h_K| \le q^\eta$ with $Q_1 \cdots Q_K \le 2^{K_0}
q^L/P$, if $\operatorname{Res}_L(\log_q P; \log_q Q_1, \dots, \log_q Q_K)$ holds, then
\[
  \sum_{\chi \in \mathcal X^*} |W(\chi)| \prod_{j=1}^K |S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le q^{L - 1/50000} \Bigl(\frac VP
  \Bigr)^{1/2}.
\]
\end{theorem}

As after Theorem~\ref{thm:type2}, the trivial character's term is at most a constant times $q^L(V/P)^{1/2}$, and the
bound saves $q^{-1/50000}$ against it. The proof is that of Proposition~\ref{prop:type2-gen}, with the classes in place
of the bins: each class satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:class} at exponents that the condition makes fit.
Of the curve $g_{\mathrm H}$ it uses only that it is a density curve (Proposition~\ref{prop:curve}) with $g_{\mathrm H}
\le 1$ and $g_{\mathrm H}(1/2) = 1$.

\begin{proof}
Take $\delta = 10^{-6}$, $H = q^\eta$, $T = 2H + 1 \le 3q^\eta$ and the bins of Section~\ref{sec:bins}. Write $m =
\log_q P$ and $s_j = \log_q Q_j \ge e_1$. As there, for large $q$ each $Q_j \ge 4$ and $Q_j \le q^{1 + L}$, and in the
bin $i$ every $|S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le 2Q_j^{\beta_{i+1} + 2\delta}$. Let $F_0$ bound $|f_0|$, $k_1 = \lceil 1/e_1
\rceil + 1$, $k_2 = \lceil 5/(2e_1) \rceil + 1$, and let $C_{\mathrm{lv}}$ be the constant of
Lemma~\ref{lem:lv-powers} at $k_{\max} = k_2$ and $F_1 = \max(F_0, 1)$, for $q$ beyond the lemma's $q_0$; let $C_d$ be
the largest of $C_{\mathrm{lv}}$, $1$, the constant of the density curve $g_{\mathrm H}$, and the constants of
Lemma~\ref{lem:moments} for $1 \le k \le k_1$.

Fix a class $c = (i, \ell_1, \dots, \ell_K)$, and let $t_j = \min(\beta_{i+1} + 2\delta, \tau(\ell_j + 1))$ if
$\ell_j < 10^6$ and $t_j = \beta_{i+1} + 2\delta$ if $\ell_j = 10^6$. Then $t_j \le \beta_i + 1/1200000 + 2 \cdot
10^{-6} \le \beta_i + 4 \cdot 10^{-6}$.

\emph{The rest.} On the class, $|S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)| \le 2Q_j^{t_j}$, by the bin and by the level. So the product is
at most $2^K q^{\sum_j s_j t_j} = 2^K q^{\sum_j s_j(t_j - 1)} \prod_j Q_j \le 4^{K_0} q^E$, with $E = \sum_j s_j(t_j -
1) + L - m$, since $\prod_j Q_j \le 2^{K_0} q^{L - m}$.

\emph{The count.} Let $\gamma$ be the least of $g_{\mathrm H}(\beta_i)$, of $\mathfrak m_k(s_j, \tau\ell_j)$ for $1 \le
k \le k_1$, and of $\mathfrak v_k(s_j, \tau\ell_j)$ for $1 \le k \le k_2$, over the $j$ with $\ell_j \ge 1$. The class
lies in the set that the least of these counts: the bin $i$, with at most $C_d (qT)^{g_{\mathrm H}(\beta_i)} (1 + \log
qT)^{10}$ characters (Section~\ref{sec:bins}), or, for a $j$ with $\ell_j \ge 1$, the characters with $|S_{Q_j}(\chi,
h_j)| \ge Q_j^{\tau\ell_j}$, counted by Lemma~\ref{lem:moments} or Lemma~\ref{lem:lv-powers}. In the last two cases
$\gamma \ge 0$, since $s_j \ge 0$ and $\tau\ell_j \le 1$, so $q^\gamma \le (qT)^\gamma$. In every case the class has
at most $C_d (qT)^\gamma (1 + \log qT)^{10}$ characters, and $\gamma \le g_{\mathrm H}(\beta_i) \le 1$. The counts are
not combined: the class is counted by the single least of them.

\emph{The condition.} The tuple $(\beta_i, \gamma, t_1, \dots, t_K)$ is an admissible tuple at $(s_1, \dots, s_K)$ in
the sense of Definition~\ref{def:res}. It remains to see that $\gamma \le \mathfrak m_k(s_j, t_j - \tau)$ and $\gamma
\le \mathfrak v_k(s_j, t_j - \tau)$ for every $j$ and every $k \ge 1$. If $\ell_j = 0$, then $t_j - \tau \le 0$, so
$\mathfrak m_k(s_j, t_j - \tau) \ge 1 \ge \gamma$ and $\mathfrak v_k(s_j, t_j - \tau) = 1$. If $\ell_j \ge 1$, then
$t_j - \tau \le \tau\ell_j$ (at the cap because $\beta_{i+1} + 2\delta - \tau \le 1$), and $\mathfrak m_k$ and
$\mathfrak v_k$ do not increase in their second argument, so it suffices that $\gamma \le \mathfrak m_k(s_j,
\tau\ell_j)$ and $\gamma \le \mathfrak v_k(s_j, \tau\ell_j)$. For $k \le k_1$ and $k \le k_2$ this is the choice of
$\gamma$. For $k > k_1$, $k_0 = \lceil 1/s_j \rceil \le \lceil 1/e_1 \rceil < k_1$ has $k_0 s_j \ge 1$, and for $u \le
1$, $\mathfrak m_k(s, u) = 2ks(1 - u)$ does not decrease in $k \ge k_0$, so $\mathfrak m_k(s_j, \tau\ell_j) \ge
\mathfrak m_{k_0}(s_j, \tau\ell_j) \ge \gamma$. For $k > k_2$, $ks_j \ge ke_1 \ge 5/2$, so $\mathfrak v_k(s_j,
\tau\ell_j) = 1 \ge \gamma$. By $\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1, \dots, s_K)$ the exponents $(E, \gamma, m)$ fit at $L$.

\emph{The sum.} The kernel bound of Section~\ref{sec:two-bounds} gives $\mathcal H$ as in Lemma~\ref{lem:class}, with a
constant $C_K$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:class} with $C_0 = 4^{K_0}$ and this $C_d$, applied to the characters of $\mathcal
X^*$ in the class, each class contributes at most $C'(1 + \log qT)^{10} q^{L - 1/25000 + \eta}(V/P)^{1/2}$, with $C'$
independent of $q$. There are at most $I(10^6 + 1)^{K_0}$ classes, and $1 + \log qT \le 3(1 + \log q)$. As in the proof
of Proposition~\ref{prop:type2-gen}, for large $q$ the number of classes times $C'(1 + \log qT)^{10}$ is at most
$q^{1/100000}$, and $L - 1/25000 + \eta + 1/100000 \le L - 1/50000$ since $\eta \le 10^{-5}$.
\end{proof}

The heuristic remark at the beginning of the section and Theorem~\ref{thm:window} are related as follows. On a class,
the moment with the power $k_j$ gives $\gamma \le \mathfrak m_{k_j}(s_j, t_j - \tau) = 2k_js_j(1 - t_j + \tau)$, that
is $s_j(1 - t_j) \ge \gamma/(2k_j) - \tau s_j$, and the half-plane gives $s_j(1 - t_j) \ge s_j(1/12 - 4 \cdot
10^{-6})$. Taking $a_j$ times the first bound plus $1 - a_j$ times the second, with the $a_j$ of the remark, shows
that for $m \ge 1$ the first inequality of Definition~\ref{def:fit} holds at every admissible tuple once $(1/12 - 4
\cdot 10^{-6})(\sum_j 1/k_j - 1) \ge 1/25000 + \tau \sum_j s_j$, for instance when $\sum_j 1/k_j \ge 1 + 6 \cdot
10^{-4}$ and $\sum_j s_j \le 5/2$ (an elementary check, not in the library and not used). So, up to the margins, the
rests of the remark pass, and Definition~\ref{def:res} admits more: the density curve's count where the zeros are rare,
the large values
of a power whose length $q^{ks}$ lies between $q^{2/3}$ and $q^{5/2}$, and the Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality where
the variable is shorter than $q$. Which boxes of the sieve satisfy the condition is decided by the tests of
Section~\ref{sec:window-tests}.

\subsection{Terms with a rest}\label{sec:window-terms}

Theorem~\ref{thm:window} bounds the sum over $\chi \in \mathcal X^*$ of $|W(\chi)| \prod_j |S_{Q_j}(\chi, h_j)|$, for
one Dirichlet polynomial $W(\chi)$, the variable, and the prime sums of a rest, where $\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1,
\dots, s_K)$ holds (Definition~\ref{def:res}). This subsection carries it to the sieve's terms, as
Lemmas~\ref{lem:family-two} to~\ref{lem:term-two} carry Theorem~\ref{thm:type2}. The comparisons, $N_1 = \lfloor x
\rfloor$, $N_2 = \lfloor 2x \rfloor$ and $\vartheta$ are those of Section~\ref{sec:type1}, and $x = q^L$.

For a $k$-tuple $\mathbf z = (z_1, \dots, z_k)$ of primes and $K$ distinct indices $i_1, \dots, i_K$ of $\{1, \dots,
k\}$, with $\mathcal S_r = \{i_1, \dots, i_K\}$, the \emph{rest} is the primes $z_{i_1}, \dots, z_{i_K}$, and the
\emph{variable} is the product $\prod_{t \notin \mathcal S_r} z_t$ of the other entries. Each prime of the rest gives a
prime sum of its own, at its dyadic scale $Q_j$, and the variable gives the polynomial $W$ over the values of the
product. For a term $\sum_{\mathbf r} (S(\mathcal A_r, r_k) - S(\mathcal B_r, r_k)/\varphi(q))$ over chains $r_1 >
\dots > r_k$, $r = r_1 \cdots r_k$, the rest is a set of the chain's primes, and the variable is the integer $n$ counted
divided by the rest: the other primes of the chain times the cofactor $n/r$. Two things differ from
Section~\ref{sec:type1}. There is one variable, not two, so no condition is ever between two variables. And
$\operatorname{Res}_L$ reads the exponent of each prime sum of the rest, not only their total, and it is asked only
above lower bounds $\sigma_j$ that the primes of the rest respect.

\begin{definition}\label{def:res-quadrant}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.ResBox
Let $L$ be real, $K \ge 1$ an integer, and $m_0, \sigma_1, \dots, \sigma_K$ reals. The condition
$\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds \emph{on the quadrant} $(m_0; \sigma_1, \dots, \sigma_K)$ if
$\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1, \dots, s_K)$ holds for all reals $m, s_1, \dots, s_K$ with
\begin{gather*}
  m \ge m_0, \qquad s_j \ge \sigma_j \ (1 \le j \le K), \\
  \sum_{j = 1}^K s_j \le L, \qquad \sum_{j = 1}^K s_j + m \le L + 10^{-5}.
\end{gather*}
\end{definition}

The quadrant is bounded below by $m_0$ and the $\sigma_j$. Its only upper bounds, $\sum_j s_j \le L$ and $\sum_j s_j +
m \le L + 10^{-5}$, are those that the dyadic scales of a family give: the rest and the variable make up $x = q^L$,
up to a bounded power of $2$, at most $q^{10^{-5}}$.

\begin{definition}\label{def:tuple-estimate}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.FullEst
Let $L$ and $e_0$ be reals, $K \ge 1$ an integer, and $\mathcal G = (\mathcal G_q)_{q \ge 1}$ a family of sets of
$K$-tuples of non-negative integers, a \emph{region} of the rest, which may depend on $q$. The \emph{tuple estimate}
holds for $\mathcal G$ at $(L, e_0)$ if for all integers $k \ge 1$ and $J_0 \ge 0$ and every real $\Lambda_1 \ge 0$
there
are $\delta > 0$ and $q_0$ such that the following holds for every $q \ge q_0$ and every $a$ prime to $q$. Let $i_1,
\dots, i_K$ be distinct indices of $\{1, \dots, k\}$, and let $\mathbf z$ run over the $k$-tuples of primes up to $N_2$
that satisfy at most $J_0$ comparisons with constant terms at most $\Lambda_1 \log q$. If every tuple of primes that
satisfies the comparisons has $z_t \ge q^{e_0}$ for every $t$, $(z_{i_1}, \dots, z_{i_K}) \in \mathcal G_q$ and $N_1 <
z_1 \cdots z_k \le N_2$, then
\[
  \Bigl|\sum_{\mathbf z} \vartheta(z_1 \cdots z_k)\Bigr| \le \frac{q^{L - \delta}}{\varphi(q)}.
\]
\end{definition}

In the tuple estimate $\delta$ may depend on the length $k$, as well as on $J_0$ and $\Lambda_1$; this is needed
because a term's cofactor adds primes to its chain. It does not depend on the indices or on the comparisons
themselves, which are chosen after $q$.

\begin{proposition}[tuples of primes with a rest]\label{prop:tuples-rest}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.full_res_exp (from SieveTypeIIHM.full_res_bound, SieveTypeIIHM.chain_res_core,
% SieveTypeIIHM.box_res_gen, SieveUnwGen.unw_gen_lo)
Assume Inputs~\ref{inp:half-plane} and~\ref{inp:cgl}. Let $0 < L \le 5/2$ and $e_0 > 0$, let $k \ge 1$ and $J_0 \ge 0$
be integers, and
let $\Lambda_1 \ge 0$, $\rho_2 \ge 0$ and $\varepsilon > 0$ be reals with $L - \rho_2 - \varepsilon > 0$. There are
$\delta > 0$ and $q_0$ such that the following holds for every $q \ge q_0$ and every $a$ prime to $q$. Let $K \ge 1$,
let
$i_1, \dots, i_K$ be distinct indices of $\{1, \dots, k\}$, and let $\sigma_1, \dots, \sigma_K$ and $\rho_1$ be reals
such that $\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds on the quadrant $(L - \rho_2 - \varepsilon; \sigma_1 - \varepsilon, \dots,
\sigma_K - \varepsilon)$. Let $\mathbf z$ run over the $k$-tuples of primes up to $N_2$ that satisfy at most $J_0$
comparisons with constant terms at most $\Lambda_1 \log q$, and suppose that every tuple of primes that satisfies the
comparisons has
\begin{gather*}
  z_t \ge q^{e_0} \ (1 \le t \le k), \qquad z_{i_j} \ge q^{\sigma_j} \ (1 \le j \le K), \\
  q^{\rho_1} \le \prod_{j = 1}^K z_{i_j} \le q^{\rho_2}, \qquad N_1 < z_1 \cdots z_k \le N_2.
\end{gather*}
Then
\[
  \Bigl|\sum_{\mathbf z} \vartheta(z_1 \cdots z_k)\Bigr| \le \frac{q^{L - \delta}}{\varphi(q)}.
\]
\end{proposition}

The number $\delta$ does not depend on $K$, the indices, the $\sigma_j$, $\rho_1$ or the comparisons. So, for fixed
$K$, $\sigma_j$ and $\rho_1$ with $\operatorname{Res}_L$ on that quadrant, the proposition says that the tuple
estimate holds at $(L, e_0)$ for the region of the $K$-tuples $\mathbf y$ with $y_j \ge q^{\sigma_j}$ and $q^{\rho_1}
\le y_1 \cdots y_K \le q^{\rho_2}$; this rewording is immediate and is not a separate theorem of the library. The margin
$\varepsilon$ is spent twice, on the variable's lower end and on the rest's dyadic scales, and $\rho_1$ does not enter
the condition.

The proof is in \S S7; here is its plan. It is that of Lemma~\ref{lem:chains-two} with one variable, the integer $\nu =
\prod_{t \notin \mathcal S_r} z_t$. Group the tuples of the variable by their product: at most $(k + 1)^k$ tuples have a
given product, so a polynomial over them is a polynomial over the integers with coefficients at most $(k + 1)^k$, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:window} bounds it, block by dyadic block of $\nu$, at each frequency of the separation of variables. A
comparison with a nonzero coefficient at an index of $\mathcal S_r$ has a prime of the rest as a pivot. Every other
comparison is a condition on the variable's tuple alone, and goes into the set over which the polynomial runs. So
nothing replaces Lemma~\ref{lem:cross}: with one variable there is no condition between two variables, and the
coefficients lose a constant, not a power of $\log q$. The argument of Lemma~\ref{lem:family-sharp-two} then applies,
with one change: a dyadic piece in which the scale of a prime of the rest is below $q^{\sigma_j - \varepsilon}$ is
empty, so the condition is asked only at scales above it. The hypotheses $q^{\rho_1} \le \prod_j z_{i_j} \le q^{\rho_2}$
and $N_1 < z_1 \cdots z_k$ put the variable above $\lfloor N_1/\lfloor q^{\rho_2} \rfloor \rfloor \ge q^{L - \rho_2 -
\varepsilon}$, and the scales of a nonempty piece have $\sum_j s_j \le L$ and $\sum_j s_j + m \le L + 10^{-5}$ for large
$q$: the quadrant of the hypothesis.

\begin{lemma}[a term with a rest]\label{lem:term-rest}
% Lean: (a) SieveTypeIIHM.chain_res_gen; (b) SieveTypeIIHM.chain_res_exp
Let $2 < L \le 5/2$, $e_0 > 0$, let $k \ge 1$ and $J_0 \ge 0$ be integers, and $\Lambda_1 \ge 0$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Let $K \ge 1$ and let $\mathcal G$ be a region of $K$-tuples for which the tuple estimate holds at $(L,
  e_0)$. There are $\delta > 0$ and $q_0$ such that the following holds for every $q \ge q_0$ and every $a$ prime to
  $q$. Let $i_1, \dots, i_K$ be distinct indices of $\{1, \dots, k\}$, and let $(r_1, \dots, r_k)$ run over the
  $k$-tuples of primes up to $N_2$ that satisfy at most $J_0$ comparisons with constant terms at most $\Lambda_1 \log
  q$. Suppose that every tuple of primes that satisfies the comparisons is strictly decreasing, has $r_i \ge q^{e_0}$
  for every $i$, and has $(r_{i_1}, \dots, r_{i_K}) \in \mathcal G_q$. Then, with $r = r_1 \cdots r_k$,
  \[
    \Bigl|\sum_{(r_1, \dots, r_k)} \Bigl(S(\mathcal A_r, r_k) - \frac{S(\mathcal B_r, r_k)}{\varphi(q)}\Bigr)\Bigr|
    \le \frac{q^{L - \delta}}{\varphi(q)}.
  \]
\item[(b)] Assume Inputs~\ref{inp:half-plane} and~\ref{inp:cgl}, and let $\rho_2 \ge 0$ and $\varepsilon > 0$ with $L -
\rho_2 -
  \varepsilon > 0$. There are $\delta > 0$ and $q_0$ such that the following holds for every $q \ge q_0$ and every $a$
  prime to $q$. Let $K \ge 1$, let $i_1, \dots, i_K$ be distinct indices of $\{1, \dots, k\}$, and let $\sigma_1,
  \dots, \sigma_K$ and $\rho_1$ be reals such that $\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds on the quadrant $(L - \rho_2 -
  \varepsilon; \sigma_1 - \varepsilon, \dots, \sigma_K - \varepsilon)$. Let the chains run as in (a), and suppose that
  every tuple of primes that satisfies the comparisons is strictly decreasing, has $r_i \ge q^{e_0}$ for every $i$,
  $r_{i_j} \ge q^{\sigma_j}$ for every $j$, and $q^{\rho_1} \le \prod_j r_{i_j} \le q^{\rho_2}$. Then the bound of (a)
  holds.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

The rest is a set of the chain's primes; the variable is everything else of $n$, the cofactor included, and no
hypothesis bounds it from above. In (b), as in Proposition~\ref{prop:tuples-rest}, $\delta$ does not depend on $K$, the
indices, the $\sigma_j$ or $\rho_1$; in (a) the region, and with it $K$, is fixed before $\delta$. So (b) is not a case
of (a) as stated, and the library proves it by the same argument, with Proposition~\ref{prop:tuples-rest} in place of
the tuple estimate.

\begin{proof}
The proof is that of Lemma~\ref{lem:term-two}, with the tuple estimate in place of Lemma~\ref{lem:chains-two}. Let
$t_{\max} = \lceil (L + 1)/e_0 \rceil$. A prime $p \ge q^{e_0}$ has $p^{t_{\max} + 1} \ge q^{L + 1} > N_2$ for $q \ge
3$. The cofactor $n/r$ of an integer counted for a chain has no prime factor below $r_k$, since $r$ is $r_k$-rough (the
chain decreases), and is at most $N_2$; so it is the product of a unique non-increasing tuple $y_1 \ge \dots \ge y_t \ge
r_k$ of primes with $t \le t_{\max}$, the cofactor $1$ being $t = 0$. For each $t$, the pairs $(\mathbf r, \mathbf y)$
are the $(k + t)$-tuples of primes up to $N_2$ that satisfy the given comparisons on $\mathbf r$, the comparisons $y_b
\le y_a$ for $a \le b$ and $y_a \ge r_k$, and $N_1 < \prod \mathbf r \prod \mathbf y \le N_2$: at most $J_0 + t^2 + t +
2 \le J_0 + t_{\max}^2 + t_{\max} + 2$ comparisons, with constant terms at most $(\Lambda_1 + L + 1)\log q$, since $\log
N_2 \le (L + 1)\log q$. Every such tuple has its entries at least $q^{e_0}$, as $y_a \ge r_k$, its rest $(r_{i_1},
\dots, r_{i_K})$ in $\mathcal G_q$, and its product in $(N_1, N_2]$. Apply the tuple estimate with $k + t$ entries, $J_0
+ t_{\max}^2 + t_{\max} + 2$ comparisons and $\Lambda_1 + L + 1$, the rest at the indices $i_1, \dots, i_K$ of the
chain; it gives $\delta_t$, and each $t$ contributes at most $q^{L - \delta_t}/\varphi(q)$. With $\delta_0 = \min_{t \le
t_{\max}} \delta_t$, the sum over the $t_{\max} + 1$ values of $t$ is at most $(t_{\max} + 1) q^{L -
\delta_0}/\varphi(q) \le q^{L - \delta_0/2}/\varphi(q)$ for large $q$, and $\delta = \delta_0/2$ will do. For (b) the
same with Proposition~\ref{prop:tuples-rest}, at $k + t$ entries, $J_0 + t_{\max}^2 + t_{\max} + 2$ comparisons,
$\Lambda_1 + L + 1$, $\rho_2$ and $\varepsilon$, in place of the tuple estimate; its hypotheses on the rest are those on
the chain, since the rest's entries are the chain's.
\end{proof}

When every counted integer's cofactor is one prime $y$, the cofactor can be in the rest too. This is what a box whose
cells leave room for exactly one more prime gives, and its cell is then known.

\begin{lemma}[the cofactor in the rest]\label{lem:term-rest-cof}
% Lean: (a) SieveTypeIIHM.chain_res_cofG; (b) SieveTypeIIHM.chain_res_cof; (c) SieveAd.cof_facts
Let $2 < L \le 5/2$, $e_0 > 0$, let $k \ge 1$ and $J_0 \ge 0$ be integers, and $\Lambda_1 \ge 0$. In (a) and (b), once
$q$ is given, let $(r_1, \dots, r_k)$ run over the $k$-tuples of primes up to $N_2$ that satisfy at most $J_0$
comparisons with constant terms at most $\Lambda_1 \log q$, and let $r = r_1 \cdots r_k$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Let $K \ge 1$ and let $\mathcal G$ be a region of $(K + 1)$-tuples for which the tuple estimate holds at
  $(L, e_0)$. There are $\delta > 0$ and $q_0$ such that for every $q \ge q_0$, every $a$ prime to $q$ and all
  distinct indices $i_1, \dots, i_K$ of $\{1, \dots, k\}$ the bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:term-rest}(a) holds if every
  tuple of primes that satisfies the comparisons is strictly decreasing, has $r_i \ge q^{e_0}$ for every $i$, $r \le
  N_1$ and $N_2 < r r_k^2$, and every integer $y \ge r_k$ with $N_1 < ry \le N_2$ has $(r_{i_1}, \dots, r_{i_K}, y)
  \in \mathcal G_q$.
\item[(b)] Assume Inputs~\ref{inp:half-plane} and~\ref{inp:cgl}, and let $\rho_2 \ge 0$ and $\varepsilon > 0$ with $L -
\rho_2 -
  \varepsilon > 0$. There are $\delta > 0$ and $q_0$ such that the following holds for every $q \ge q_0$ and every $a$
  prime to $q$. Let $K \ge 1$, let $i_1, \dots, i_K$ be distinct indices of $\{1, \dots, k\}$, and let $\sigma_1,
  \dots, \sigma_{K + 1}$ and $\rho_1$ be reals such that $\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds on the quadrant $(L - \rho_2 -
  \varepsilon; \sigma_1 - \varepsilon, \dots, \sigma_{K + 1} - \varepsilon)$, of $K + 1$ prime sums. The bound of
  Lemma~\ref{lem:term-rest}(a) holds if every tuple of primes that satisfies the comparisons is strictly decreasing,
  has $r_i \ge q^{e_0}$ for every $i$, $r_{i_j} \ge q^{\sigma_j}$ for $j \le K$, $r \le N_1$ and $N_2 < r r_k^2$,
  and every integer $y \ge r_k$ with $N_1 < ry \le N_2$ has $y \ge q^{\sigma_{K + 1}}$ and $q^{\rho_1} \le y
  \prod_{j \le K} r_{i_j} \le q^{\rho_2}$.
\item[(c)] Let $x^{1/D} \ge 3$, and let $\mathfrak b$ be a box of $k$ cells with $l_i \le h_i$ for every $i$,
$h(\mathfrak
  b) + 2 \le D$ and $l(\mathfrak b) + 2 l_k \ge D + 1$. Let $p_1, \dots, p_k$ be integers with $x^{l_i/D} \le p_i <
  x^{h_i/D}$, and $r = p_1 \cdots p_k$. Then $r \le N_1$, $N_2 < r p_k^2$, and every integer $y \ge p_k$ with $N_1 < ry
  \le N_2$ has
  \[
    x^{\max(D - h(\mathfrak b) - 1,\, l_k)/D} \le y \le x^{(D - l(\mathfrak b) + 1)/D}.
  \]
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

In (a) and (b) the conditions $r \le N_1$ and $N_2 < r r_k^2$ make the cofactor one prime, and the rest has $K + 1$
members, the last the cofactor; (c) gives those conditions for a box of the main tree, and the cofactor's cell
$(\max(D - h(\mathfrak b) - 1, l_k), D - l(\mathfrak b) + 1)$, its upper end included.

\begin{proof}
(a) and (b). As in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:term-rest}, the integers counted for a chain are $n = r y_1 \cdots y_t$
with $y_1 \ge \dots \ge y_t \ge r_k$ primes. Only $t = 1$ remains: $t = 0$ gives $n = r \le N_1$, outside $(N_1, N_2]$,
and $t \ge 2$ gives $n \ge r r_k^2 > N_2$. The pairs $(\mathbf r, y)$ are the $(k + 1)$-tuples of primes up to $N_2$
that satisfy the given comparisons, $y \ge r_k$ and $N_1 < ry \le N_2$: at most $J_0 + 4$ comparisons, with constant
terms at most $(\Lambda_1 + L + 1)\log q$. Each has its entries at least $q^{e_0}$, and, by the hypothesis on $y$, its
rest $(r_{i_1}, \dots, r_{i_K}, y)$, at the indices $i_1, \dots, i_K, k + 1$, in $\mathcal G_q$ for (a), and with the
lower bounds $\sigma_j$ and the product in $[q^{\rho_1}, q^{\rho_2}]$ for (b). The tuple estimate with $k + 1$ entries,
$J_0 + 4$ comparisons and $\Lambda_1 + L + 1$, or Proposition~\ref{prop:tuples-rest} with these and $\rho_2$,
$\varepsilon$, bounds the sum; $\delta$ is theirs.

(c). Since $x^{1/D} \ge 3$, $x^{(D - 1)/D} \le N_1$ and $N_2 < x^{(D + 1)/D}$; and $x^{l(\mathfrak b)/D} \le r \le
x^{h(\mathfrak b)/D}$. So $r \le x^{(D - 1)/D} \le N_1$, as $h(\mathfrak b) \le D - 2$; and $r p_k^2 \ge x^{(l(\mathfrak
b) + 2l_k)/D} \ge x^{(D + 1)/D} > N_2$. Let $y \ge p_k$ with $N_1 < ry \le N_2$. Then $y \ge p_k \ge x^{l_k/D}$; if $y
< x^{(D - h(\mathfrak b) - 1)/D}$, then $ry < x^{(D - 1)/D} \le N_1$, which is false; and if $y > x^{(D - l(\mathfrak b)
+ 1)/D}$, then $ry > x^{(D + 1)/D} > N_2$, which is false.
\end{proof}

Section~\ref{sec:window-tests} evaluates the terms of boxes with these lemmas: the test (W) through
Lemmas~\ref{lem:term-rest}(b) and~\ref{lem:term-rest-cof}(b), with $\operatorname{Res}_L$ on a quadrant, and the test
(W$'$) through Lemmas~\ref{lem:term-rest}(a) and~\ref{lem:term-rest-cof}(a), with the tuple estimate of
Lemma~\ref{lem:tied-to-tuples} for the box of the rest's cells.

\subsection{The two corners of a box}\label{sec:corners}

A box of the decomposition gives each prime of the rest a cell, so each exponent $s_j$ an interval $[a_j, b_j]$, and
the rest and the variable make up $q^L$: on the box $s_1 + \dots + s_K + m$ is $L$ up to the dyadic blocks. The
condition on a quadrant (Definition~\ref{def:res-quadrant}) asks $\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1, \dots, s_K)$ at every
$s_j
\ge \sigma_j = a_j$ and every $m \ge m_0$, where $m_0$ is the variable that the upper ends leave, about $L - \sum_j
b_j$. It contains the corner $(a_1, \dots, a_K; m_0)$, where the prime sums are shortest and so is the variable, and
which no tuple of the box reaches: there $\sum_j s_j + m$ falls short of $L$ by the box's width $\sum_j (b_j - a_j)$.
On a wide box the condition can fail at that corner and hold at every point that the box's tuples reach (a
heuristic: the library proves no such failure). The condition below ties the variable to the rest.

\begin{definition}\label{def:res-tied}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.ResBoxT
Let $L$ and $w$ be real, $K \ge 1$, and $a_j, b_j$ real for $1 \le j \le K$. The condition $\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds
\emph{on the box $\prod_j [a_j, b_j]$ with the variable tied to the rest}, with the margin $w$, if
$\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1, \dots, s_K)$ holds for all reals $s_j \in [a_j, b_j]$ and $m$ with
\[
  L - w \le s_1 + \dots + s_K + m \le L + 10^{-5}.
\]
\end{definition}

The test (W$'$) of Definition~\ref{def:window-tests} gives this condition with the margin $w = 2 \cdot 10^{-5}$, on
the cells widened by $2 \cdot 10^{-5}$ at each end; Lemma~\ref{lem:tied-to-tuples} takes it in that form.

\emph{The clamp.} For an admissible tuple, write $Y = \sum_j s_j(t_j - 1)$ for the rest's term, so that the exponent of
Definition~\ref{def:fit} is $E = Y + L - m$. The variable enters through the \emph{clamp}
\begin{gather*}
  \operatorname{cl}(\gamma, m) = \min\Bigl(\frac{\max(1 - m, 0)}2,\ \max\Bigl(0, \frac\gamma2 + y_0(m)\Bigr)\Bigr), \\
  y_0(m) = \frac12\Bigl(\frac5{12} + \delta' + 2\varepsilon' - m\Bigl(\frac{11}{12} + \delta'\Bigr)\Bigr),
\end{gather*}
with $\delta' = \varepsilon' = 1/1000$, so $y_0(m) = (1259 - 2753m)/6000$: the first entry is orthogonality's, the
second the kernel bound's. The clamp is non-negative, and it is $0$ for $m \ge 1$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:clamp}
% Lean: SieveResTest.binOK_of, SieveResTest.clampR, SieveResTest.y0R
Let $L$, $Y$, $\gamma$ and $m$ be real. If $Y + \gamma/2 + \operatorname{cl}(\gamma, m) \le -1/25000$, then
the exponents $(Y + L - m, \gamma, m)$ fit at $L$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $E = Y + L - m$. If the clamp is its first entry, then, as $(m + \max(m, 1))/2 - m = \max(1 - m, 0)/2$,
$E + \gamma/2 + (m + \max(m, 1))/2 = L + Y + \gamma/2 + \operatorname{cl}(\gamma, m) \le L - 1/25000$: the first
alternative of Definition~\ref{def:fit}. Otherwise the clamp is $\max(0, \gamma/2 + y_0(m))$, at least $0$ and at least
$\gamma/2 + y_0(m)$. The first gives $E + \gamma/2 + m \le L - 1/25000$. Since $b(m) - m = 5/12 + \delta' +
2\varepsilon'
- m(11/12 + \delta')$, $E + m/2 + \gamma + b(m)/2 = L + Y + \gamma/2 + (\gamma/2 + y_0(m))$, and the second gives
that this is at most $L - 1/25000$: the second alternative.
\end{proof}

\emph{The bound at a corner.} The test (W$'$) (Section~\ref{sec:window-tests}) checks the inequality of this lemma at
two corners of a box, in fixed-point arithmetic with the unit $2^{-64}$ (\S S7). In it the large values
(Lemma~\ref{lem:lv-powers}) enter through two functions. Let $\tau = 10^{-6}$, $\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}} = 10^{-5}$ and,
for $k \ge 1$,
\begin{multline*}
  \operatorname{LV}_k(\gamma; e) = \max\Bigl(-e\Bigl(\frac3{10} - \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}\Bigr),
  \frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}} - \gamma}{2k}, \\
  \frac{1/2 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}} - \gamma - ke}{4k}, \frac{4/3 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}} - \gamma - 2ke}{4k}\Bigr),
\end{multline*}
\begin{multline*}
  \Gamma_k(e_-, e_+) = \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}} + \max\Bigl(2ke_+\Bigl(\frac15 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}\Bigr),
  \frac12 - ke_-\Bigl(\frac15 - 4\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}\Bigr), \\
  \frac43 - ke_-\Bigl(\frac65 - 4\epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}\Bigr)\Bigr).
\end{multline*}
For $k \ge 1$ and $0 \le e_- \le s \le e_+$ with $ke_- \ge 2/3 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$, if $\gamma > \Gamma_k(e_-,
e_+)$, then every size $t$ with $\gamma \le \mathfrak v_k(s, t - \tau)$ has $s(t - 1) - s\tau \le
\operatorname{LV}_k(\gamma; e_-)$ (\S S7; Lean \lean{SieveResTest.\allowbreak lv\_bound}).

The test applies this to each member of the rest through a \emph{large values' condition}, which depends on an
exponent $e$ and an interval $[e_-, e_+]$ of thresholds. Let $k_0 = \lceil 1/e \rceil$ ($k_0 = 0$ if $e = 0$), and take
the two powers $k = k_0 - 1$ and $k = k_0 - 2$. For such a power and an interval $[\alpha, \alpha'] \subseteq [0, 1]$
with ends in $2^{-64}\mathbb Z$, let $U$ be an upward rounding of $\operatorname{LV}_k(\cdot\,; e)$ in the fixed point
(Lean \lean{SieveResTest.\allowbreak lvVal}) if $k \ge 1$, $ke_- \ge 2/3 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$ and $\alpha$ exceeds
an upward rounding of $\Gamma_k(e_-, e_+)$ (Lean \lean{SieveResTest.\allowbreak lvG}), and let $U = 0$ otherwise. The
condition $\mathfrak L(s, t, \gamma)$ asks that, for both powers and every such interval that contains $\gamma$, $s(t -
1) - s\tau$ be at most the chord of $U$ between $\alpha$ and $\alpha'$, taken at $\gamma$. Of the roundings the proofs
below use only that they are at least $\operatorname{LV}_k(\cdot\,; e)$ and $\Gamma_k(e_-, e_+)$ (Lean
\lean{SieveResTest.\allowbreak lvVal\_ge}, \lean{SieveResTest.\allowbreak lvGv\_ge}). For a cell $[a, b]$ with ends in
$2^{-64}\mathbb Z$ and $b > 0$, the condition \emph{at the upper end}, $\mathfrak L^+_{a,b}$, has $e = b$ and $[e_-,
e_+] = [a, b]$, the thresholds of the cell. For $v \in 2^{-64}\mathbb Z$, the condition \emph{at the lower end},
$\mathfrak L^-_{a,v}$, has $e = a$ and $[e_-, e_+] = [a, a + v]$.

Let $m_+$ be real, $e_1, \dots, e_K \ge 0$ and $\mathfrak L_1, \dots, \mathfrak L_K$ such conditions. The \emph{bound
holds from $m_+$ on, at the exponents $e_j$, with the conditions $\mathfrak L_j$}, if
\[
  \sum_{j=1}^K s_j(t_j - 1) + \frac\gamma2 + \operatorname{cl}(\gamma, m) \le -\frac1{25000}
\]
for every $m \ge m_+$, all $s_j \ge e_j$ with $\sum_j s_j \le L$, and all reals $\beta, \gamma, t_1, \dots, t_K$ with
$1/2 \le \beta \le 11/12$, $\gamma \le g_{\mathrm H}(\beta)$, $t_j \le \beta + 4 \cdot 10^{-6}$, $\gamma \le \mathfrak
m_k(s_j, t_j - \tau)$ for all
$j$ and $k \ge 1$, and $\mathfrak L_j(s_j, t_j, \gamma)$ for all $j$ (Lean \lean{SieveResTest.GoalAtV}). These are the
conditions on an admissible tuple in Definition~\ref{def:res}, except that the large values enter through the
conditions $\mathfrak L_j$, not
through $\mathfrak v_k$. From $m = 1$ on the clamp is $0$, and the bound is $\sum_j s_j(t_j - 1) + \gamma/2 \le
-1/25000$.

\begin{lemma}[the two corners]\label{lem:two-corners}
% Lean: SieveResTest.two_corner, SieveResTest.clamp_step, SieveResTest.carry_term, SieveResTest.carry_le, SieveResTest.mom_carry, SieveResTest.max4_shift, SieveResTest.lvU_carry, SieveResTest.lvok_carry, SieveResTest.lvok_mkTv, SieveResTest.binOK_of
Let $L$ be real, $w \ge 0$ and $K \ge 1$. For $1 \le j \le K$ let $a_j \le b_j$ be in $2^{-64}\mathbb Z$, $a_j \ge 0$
and
$b_j > 0$, and let $v$ and $m_+ \ge 0$ be in $2^{-64}\mathbb Z$ with $b_j \le a_j + v$ for every $j$. Write $\Xi =
\sum_j (b_j - a_j)$ for the box's width. Suppose that
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(U)] the bound holds from $m_+$ on, at the exponents $b_j$, with the conditions $\mathfrak L^+_{a_j, b_j}$;
\item[(Lo)] the bound holds from $1$ on, at the exponents $a_j$, with the conditions $\mathfrak L^-_{a_j, v}$;
\item[(Rm)] $m_+ + \Xi/11 + \sum_j b_j + w \le L$.
\end{enumerate}
Then $\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1, \dots, s_K)$ holds for all reals $s_j \in [a_j, b_j]$ and $m$ with $\sum_j s_j + m
\ge
L - w$. In particular $\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds on the box $\prod_j [a_j, b_j]$ with the variable tied to the rest,
with the margin $w$.
\end{lemma}

The upper corner is $s_j = b_j$, with the variable $m_+$, shorter by at least $\Xi/11 + w$ than the upper ends leave;
the
lower corner has the conditions at the lower ends, $\mathfrak L^-_{a_j, v}$, and no clamp. The proof carries an
admissible tuple at a point $\mathbf s$
of the box to the upper corner, where its rest's term is lower by half the distance $\Theta = \sum_j (b_j - s_j)$, while
the variable at
$\mathbf s$ is longer than $m_+$ by at least $(12/11)\Theta$; the clamp falls with it.

\begin{proof}
Fix $\mathbf s$ and $m$ as in the statement and an admissible tuple $(\beta, \gamma, \mathbf t)$ at $\mathbf s$
(Definition~\ref{def:res}). By Lemma~\ref{lem:clamp} it suffices to prove $Y + \gamma/2 + \operatorname{cl}(\gamma, m)
\le
-1/25000$, $Y = \sum_j s_j(t_j - 1)$. By (Rm), $\sum_j s_j \le \sum_j b_j \le L$, and $0 \le \Theta \le \Xi$.

\emph{The lower corner.} The tuple satisfies the hypotheses of (Lo) at $\mathbf s$ and $m = 1$: $s_j \ge a_j$, $\sum_j
s_j \le L$, the moments' conditions are those of the tuple, and since $a_j \le s_j \le a_j + v$ the tuple has
$\mathfrak L^-_{a_j, v}(s_j, t_j, \gamma)$ (Lean \lean{SieveResTest.lvok\_mkTv}; \S S7). As $\operatorname{cl} \ge 0$,
\[
  Y + \gamma/2 \le -1/25000. \tag{a}
\]

\emph{The carry.} Put $t'_j = 1/2 + (s_j/b_j)(t_j - 1/2)$, so $b_j(t'_j - 1/2) = s_j(t_j - 1/2)$: the excess over the
square root is kept. The tuple $(\beta, \gamma, \mathbf t')$ of reals is checked against the hypotheses of (U) at the
exponents $b_j$:
\begin{itemize}
\item $b_j(t'_j - 1) = s_j(t_j - 1) - (b_j - s_j)/2$, so $\sum_j b_j(t'_j - 1) = Y - \Theta/2$.
\item $t'_j \le \beta + 4 \cdot 10^{-6}$: since $0 \le s_j/b_j \le 1$, $t'_j \le t_j$ if $t_j \ge 1/2$, and $t'_j \le
1/2
  \le \beta$ otherwise.
\item The moments: $kb_j(1 - 2t'_j) = ks_j(1 - 2t_j)$, so
  $\mathfrak m_k(b_j, t'_j - \tau) = \max(1, kb_j) + ks_j(1 - 2t_j) + 2\tau kb_j \ge \mathfrak m_k(s_j, t_j - \tau) \ge
  \gamma$ for every $k \ge 1$, as $s_j \le b_j$.
\item The large values: $\mathfrak L^+_{a_j, b_j}(b_j, t'_j, \gamma)$. Take a power $k$ and an interval $[\alpha,
  \alpha'] \ni \gamma$. If its function is $0$, the claim is $b_j(t'_j - 1) - b_j\tau \le 0$, true as $t'_j < 1$ by the
  second item and $\beta \le 11/12$.
  Otherwise $k \ge 1$, $ka_j \ge 2/3 + \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}$ and $\gamma \ge \alpha > \Gamma_k(a_j, b_j)$. Then $ks_j
  \ge ka_j$, and $\Gamma_k(s_j, b_j) \le \Gamma_k(a_j, b_j)$, since its second and third entries decrease in the lower
  end. The bound of the large values with $e_- = s_j$, $e_+ = b_j$ and the tuple's $\gamma \le \mathfrak v_k(s_j, t_j -
  \tau)$ gives $s_j(t_j - 1) - s_j\tau \le \operatorname{LV}_k(\gamma; s_j)$. Entry by entry,
  $\operatorname{LV}_k(\gamma;
  s_j) - (b_j - s_j)/2 \le \operatorname{LV}_k(\gamma; b_j)$: the first entry falls by $(b_j - s_j)(3/10 -
  \epsilon_{\mathrm{lv}}) \le (b_j - s_j)/2$, the second does not change, the third falls by $(b_j - s_j)/4$ and the
  fourth by $(b_j - s_j)/2$. So
  \[
    b_j(t'_j - 1) - b_j\tau \le s_j(t_j - 1) - s_j\tau - \frac{b_j - s_j}2 \le \operatorname{LV}_k(\gamma; b_j).
  \]
  As a maximum of affine functions of $\gamma$, $\operatorname{LV}_k(\cdot\,; b_j)$ is convex, so it is below its chord
  between $\alpha$ and $\alpha'$, and the rounded values at the ends are above it.
\end{itemize}
So $(\beta, \gamma, \mathbf t')$ satisfies the hypotheses of (U) at the exponents $b_j$, with $\sum_j b_j \le L$, and
$m = m_+$:
\[
  Y - \Theta/2 + \gamma/2 + \operatorname{cl}(\gamma, m_+) \le -1/25000. \tag{b}
\]

\emph{The variable.} Since $\sum_j s_j = \sum_j b_j - \Theta$, (Rm) and $\Theta \le \Xi$ give
\[
  m \ge L - w - \sum_j b_j + \Theta \ge m_+ + \frac\Xi{11} + \Theta \ge m_+ + \frac{12}{11}\Theta.
\]

\emph{The clamp falls.} Write $\operatorname{cl}_1(m) = \max(1 - m, 0)/2$ and $\operatorname{cl}_2(m) = \max(0, \gamma/2
+ y_0(m))$ for the clamp's two entries. As $m - m_+ \ge
\Theta \ge 0$, $\operatorname{cl}_1(m) \le \max(\operatorname{cl}_1(m_+) - \Theta/2, 0)$. As $(12/11)(11/12 + \delta')
\ge 1$,
\begin{multline*}
  y_0(m) = y_0(m_+) - (m - m_+)\Bigl(\frac{11}{12} + \delta'\Bigr)\Big/2 \\
  \le y_0(m_+) - \frac{12}{11}\Bigl(\frac{11}{12} + \delta'\Bigr)\frac\Theta2 \le y_0(m_+) - \frac\Theta2,
\end{multline*}
so $\operatorname{cl}_2(m) \le \max(\operatorname{cl}_2(m_+) - \Theta/2, 0)$. The clamp at $m_+$ is
$\operatorname{cl}_i(m_+)$ for some $i$, and then
$\operatorname{cl}(\gamma, m) \le \operatorname{cl}_i(m) \le \max(\operatorname{cl}(\gamma, m_+) - \Theta/2, 0)$. By (b)
in the first case
and (a) in the second, $Y + \gamma/2 + \operatorname{cl}(\gamma, m) \le -1/25000$.
\end{proof}

The upper bound $\sum_j s_j + m \le L + 10^{-5}$ is not used: a longer variable only lowers the clamp. The factor
$12/11$ is a simple value at which the clamp's flatter entry, of slope $(11/12 + \delta')/2$ in $m$, falls by at least
$\Theta/2$; the
shortening by $\Xi/11 \ge \Theta/11$ supplies the excess over $\Theta$ at every point of the box. The thresholds of the
upper corner are the cell's because the counts by large values are known only at $s_j$, anywhere in $[a_j, b_j]$.

The evaluation of a term needs the condition on a quadrant (Proposition~\ref{prop:tuples-rest}). The next lemma cuts
the box into sub-cells thin enough that on each the quadrant's condition follows from the tied one, and gives the tuple
estimate on the box. Here a tuple has $k$ primes, $K$ of which make up the rest.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:tied-to-tuples}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.fullEst_of_tight, SieveTypeIIHM.resBox_of_tight, SieveTypeIIHM.full_res_exp, SieveCurveHx.ghx_le_one, SieveCurveHx.ghx_half, SieveCurveHx.counts_hx
Under Inputs~\ref{inp:half-plane} and~\ref{inp:cgl}, let $0 < L \le 5/2$, $e_0 > 0$, $K \ge 1$, and $0 \le a_j \le b_j$
for $1 \le j \le K$ with $\sum_j b_j + 10^{-5} \le L$. If $\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds on the box $\prod_j [a_j - 2
\cdot 10^{-5}, b_j + 2 \cdot 10^{-5}]$ with the variable tied to the rest, with the margin $2 \cdot 10^{-5}$, then the
tuple estimate (Definition~\ref{def:tuple-estimate}) holds at $(L, e_0)$ for the region $\mathcal G_q$ of the
$K$-tuples $(z_{i_1}, \dots, z_{i_K})$ with $q^{a_j} \le z_{i_j} \le q^{b_j}$, $1 \le j \le K$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $k$, $J_0$ and $\Lambda_1 \ge 0$ be given. Put $\eta = 1/(4 \cdot 10^5 K)$, so $K\eta = 2.5 \cdot 10^{-6}$. For
$\mathbf n$ in the finite set $\mathcal J = \prod_j \{0, 1, \dots, \lfloor (b_j - a_j)/\eta \rfloor\}$ let
$A_j(\mathbf n) = a_j + n_j\eta \in [a_j, b_j]$, the sub-cell $[A_j, A_j + \eta)$, and $\rho_2(\mathbf n) = \sum_j
(A_j(\mathbf n) + \eta)$.

\emph{A sub-cell.} $\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds on the quadrant $(L - \rho_2(\mathbf n) - \eta; A_1 - \eta, \dots, A_K
- \eta)$ of Definition~\ref{def:res-quadrant}. Let $m \ge L - \rho_2(\mathbf n) - \eta$ and $s_j \ge A_j - \eta$ with
$\sum_j s_j + m \le L + 10^{-5}$. Then $s_j \ge a_j - 2 \cdot 10^{-5}$; and
\[
  s_j - (A_j - \eta) \le \sum_i (s_i - A_i + \eta) \le L + 10^{-5} - m - \sum_i A_i + K\eta \le 10^{-5} + 2K\eta + \eta,
\]
so $s_j \le b_j + 10^{-5} + 2K\eta \le b_j + 2 \cdot 10^{-5}$. Also $\sum_j s_j + m \ge \sum_j (A_j - \eta) + L -
\rho_2(\mathbf n) - \eta = L - 2K\eta - \eta \ge L - 2 \cdot 10^{-5}$. So $(m; \mathbf s)$ is in the widened box with
the variable tied, and $\operatorname{Res}_L(m; \mathbf s)$ holds.

\emph{The estimate of a sub-cell.} For each $\mathbf n$, Proposition~\ref{prop:tuples-rest} with $k$, $J_0 + 2K$
comparisons, $\Lambda_1 + L$, $\rho_2 = \rho_2(\mathbf n) \ge 0$, $\varepsilon = \eta$, $\sigma_j = A_j(\mathbf n)$ and
$\rho_1 = 0$ applies, since $L - \rho_2(\mathbf n) - \eta \ge L - \sum_j b_j - K\eta - \eta \ge 10^{-5} - 5 \cdot
10^{-6} > 0$; it gives $\delta_{\mathbf n} > 0$ and $q_{\mathbf n}$. Let $\delta_0 = \min_{\mathbf n} \delta_{\mathbf
n}$, and take $q_0 \ge \max_{\mathbf n} q_{\mathbf n}$ such that $|\mathcal J| \le q^{\delta_0/2}$ for $q \ge q_0$.

\emph{The cut.} Let $q \ge q_0$, $a$, the rest's indices $i_1, \dots, i_K$ and comparisons be as in
Definition~\ref{def:tuple-estimate}. Each tuple $\mathbf z$ that satisfies them has $q^{a_j} \le z_{i_j} \le q^{b_j}$,
so $n_j(\mathbf z) = \lfloor (\log_q z_{i_j} - a_j)/\eta \rfloor$ defines $\mathbf n(\mathbf z) \in \mathcal J$, and
$\mathbf n(\mathbf z) = \mathbf n$ if and only if the $2K$ comparisons $z_{i_j} \ge q^{A_j(\mathbf n)}$ and $z_{i_j} <
q^{A_j(\mathbf n) + \eta}$ hold. Their constant terms are $-A_j \log q \le 0$ and $(A_j + \eta)\log q \le L\log q$,
since $A_j + \eta \le b_j + 10^{-5} \le L$. The sum over the tuples is the sum over $\mathbf n \in \mathcal J$ of the
sums over the families with these $2K$ comparisons added. In the family of $\mathbf n$ every tuple has its entries at
least $q^{e_0}$, $z_{i_j} \ge q^{A_j}$, $1 \le \prod_j z_{i_j} \le q^{\rho_2(\mathbf n)}$ and its product in $(N_1,
N_2]$, and $\operatorname{Res}_L$ holds on the quadrant above; so its sum is at most $q^{L - \delta_{\mathbf
n}}/\varphi(q) \le q^{L - \delta_0}/\varphi(q)$. Summing over $\mathcal J$ gives at most $q^{L -
\delta_0/2}/\varphi(q)$, and $\delta = \delta_0/2$.
\end{proof}

In Lemma~\ref{lem:two-corners} the cells are the box's cells widened by $2 \cdot 10^{-5}$ and rounded outward to
$2^{-64}\mathbb Z$, so the two lemmas together turn a passing test (W$'$) into the tuple estimate on the box
(Lemma~\ref{lem:window-kernel}(b) and Lemma~\ref{lem:window-tests}).

\subsection{The tests of the window}\label{sec:window-tests}

The window evaluates the term of a box when a \emph{rest}, a set of its primes, satisfies the resonance condition
(Definition~\ref{def:res}) on the exponents that the box's cells allow. This subsection states the condition in the
units of Section~\ref{sec:decomp}: a cell $(l, h)$ is in units of $1/D$ of exponents of $x$, and $Le/D$ is the exponent
of $q$ of the unit $e$. Throughout, $\mathfrak b$ is a box of $k \ge 1$ primes with cells $(l_i, h_i)$, the curve is
$g_{\mathrm H}$, and for a nonempty set $\mathcal S_r$ of indices, $l(\mathcal S_r)$ and $h(\mathcal S_r)$ are as in
Section~\ref{sec:decomp}.

\emph{The cofactor.} The cofactor $n/(p_1 \cdots p_k)$ of a counted $n$ is one prime when the box's cells leave room
for one prime and no more:
\[
  (\mathrm{Cf}) \qquad h(\mathfrak b) \le D - 2, \qquad l(\mathfrak b) + 2l_k \ge D + 1,
\]
the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:term-rest-cof}(c). For large $q$ that lemma gives $p_1 \cdots p_k \le N_1$ and $N_2 <
p_1 \cdots p_k\, p_k^2$, and puts the cofactor of a counted $n$, a prime $y \ge p_k$, possibly equal to $p_k$, in the
closed \emph{cell} $c_* = (l_*, h_*)$ with $l_* = \max(D - h(\mathfrak b) - 1, l_k)$ and $h_* = D - l(\mathfrak b) + 1$.
Over a frozen part $\mathcal M(e_-, e_+)$ and a chain $\mathfrak c$ of $j \ge 1$ primes
with cells $(l'_i, h'_i)$, the chain's cofactor $k'$, the cofactor of $n/(R p'_1 \cdots p'_j)$, is one prime when
\[
  (\mathrm{Cf}_{\mathcal M}) \qquad e_+ + h'(\mathfrak c) \le D - 2, \qquad e_- + l'(\mathfrak c) + 2l'_j \ge D + 1,
\]
by the argument of that lemma with $R \in (x^{e_-/D}, x^{e_+/D}]$ (\lean{SieveAd.\allowbreak frozCof\_facts}), and
its cell is $c_* = (l_*, h_*)$ with $l_* = \max(D - e_+ - h'(\mathfrak c) - 1, l'_j)$ and $h_* = \max(D - e_- -
l'(\mathfrak c), 0) + 1$.
% Lean: SieveAd.cof_facts, SieveAd.frozCof_facts, SieveAd.big_facts

\begin{definition}\label{def:window-tests}
% Lean: SieveTypeIIHM.ResBox, SieveTypeIIHM.ResBoxT; the conditions as the kernel reads them: SieveAd.chkIRCH, SieveAd.chkIRY, SieveAd.chkIRFH, SieveAd.chkIRFY, SieveAd.rpass, SieveAd.rpassY, SieveAd.headLo
Let $\mathfrak b$ be a box of $k \le 13$ primes with $1 \le l_i \le h_i \le D$ for every $i$. A \emph{rest} is a
nonempty set $\mathcal S_r$ of its indices, or, when $(\mathrm{Cf})$ holds, such a set together with the cofactor, whose
cell is then $c_*$. Let $c_1, \dots, c_K$ be the lower ends of the rest's cells and $d_1, \dots, d_K$ their upper ends,
$K = |\mathcal S_r|$ or $|\mathcal S_r| + 1$, and $H = d_1 + \dots + d_K$; so $H = h(\mathcal S_r)$, or $H =
h(\mathcal S_r) + h_*$ with the cofactor. The box passes
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(W)] the \emph{test of the window} for the rest if $H + 1 < D$ and $\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1, \dots, s_K)$
  holds for every $m \ge L(D - H - 1)/D$ and every $s_j \ge L(c_j - 1)/D$ with $\sum_j s_j \le L$ and $\sum_j s_j + m
  \le L + 10^{-5}$: the condition on the quadrant (Definition~\ref{def:res-quadrant}) with the least exponent $m_0 =
  L(D - H - 1)/D$ of the variable and the lower ends $\sigma_j = L(c_j - 1)/D$ of the rest;
\item[(W$'$)] the \emph{test at the two corners} for the rest if $c_j \le d_j$ for every $j$, $\sum_j Ld_j/D + 10^{-5}
  \le L$, and $\operatorname{Res}_L(m; s_1, \dots, s_K)$ holds for every $s$ with $Lc_j/D - 2 \cdot 10^{-5} \le s_j \le
  Ld_j/D + 2 \cdot 10^{-5}$ and every $m$ with $L - 2 \cdot 10^{-5} \le \sum_j s_j + m \le L + 10^{-5}$: the tied
  condition (Definition~\ref{def:res-tied}) on the box of the rest's cells, widened by $2 \cdot 10^{-5}$, with $w = 2
  \cdot 10^{-5}$.
\end{enumerate}
Each test is \emph{with the cofactor} when the rest contains it. Let $\mathcal M = \mathcal M(e_-, e_+)$ be a frozen
part of a box of $k \ge 2$ primes, and $\mathfrak c$ a chain of $j \ge 1$ primes, with $(k - 1) + j \le 13$ and $1 \le
l \le h \le D$ for all the cells of $\mathfrak c$ and of $\mathfrak b'$. A rest is a nonempty set of the variables
$a_2, \dots, a_k$, $p'_1, \dots, p'_j$, each in its cell, or, when $(\mathrm{Cf}_{\mathcal M})$ holds, such a set
together
with the chain's cofactor $k'$, in its cell $c_*$. The chain passes (W$_{\mathcal M}$) or (W$'_{\mathcal M}$) over
$\mathcal M$ if the condition of (W) or of (W$'$) holds on the cells of the rest.
\end{definition}

In (W) the \emph{variable} is all of $n$ outside the rest: the box's other primes and, when the rest does not contain
the cofactor, the
cofactor, whatever its number of primes. Its exponent is bounded only from below, through the rest's upper ends: $n >
