them over frozen parts; the first prime's cells were planned on two machines, in about three to four hours on four
cores each. The generator predicts $273632$ seconds of one core of the kernel's time for the part files, about $76$
hours, $183$ to $240$ seconds for each file; this is its model's prediction, not a measurement. \S S5 has the
statistics in more detail.

\subsection{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:2110}}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:2110}]
Take $L = 21/10$. As in Section~\ref{sec:proof}, Theorem~\ref{thm:type2}, under Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}, gives the
comparisons of Section~\ref{sec:type1} at $L$ and with them the tests of Section~\ref{sec:decomp}.
Theorem~\ref{thm:type2-leaves}, under Inputs~\ref{inp:half-plane} and~\ref{inp:cgl}, gives the estimate in two variables
on the leaves' region $\mathcal R^{\mathrm{lf}}_L$, and with it Propositions~\ref{prop:harman-two3}
and~\ref{prop:harman-fill3} and the tests with (L) or (L$_3$), Lemma~\ref{lem:tests3}. Theorem~\ref{thm:window}, under
the same two inputs, gives the window, and with it the tests (W) and (W$'$), Lemmas~\ref{lem:window-tests}
and~\ref{lem:window-kernel}. Propositions~\ref{prop:reversal} and~\ref{prop:reversal-plus} give the reversals of
either sign. So Proposition~\ref{prop:tree-bound} and Theorem~\ref{thm:tree} hold for the trees of
Definition~\ref{def:tree-b}, and Lemma~\ref{lem:search-b} for their search. Proposition~\ref{prop:certificate-b} gives
a floor $f_*$ at which the box of no prime passes (J3), and bounds the term of the root box with the sign $-$ at
$c/\mathcal K$, with $c = 1126600180626425749 < \mathcal K = 2^{60}$. Theorem~\ref{thm:tree} gives
Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} under the two inputs.

Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} enters as in Section~\ref{sec:proof}, and through the bins of the zeros in the window of
Theorem~\ref{thm:window} and in the estimate on the leaves' region. Input~\ref{inp:cgl} enters in two places: through
the count of the large values of the powers of a prime sum, Lemma~\ref{lem:lv-powers}, in the window; and through the
curve $g^*$ of Proposition~\ref{prop:curve-star}, in the estimate on the leaves' region. Ingham's and Huxley's density
estimates enter through $g^*$ and $g_{\mathrm H}$.
\end{proof}

The remarks after the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} hold here as well: the proof does not compute $K$, no constant
is claimed to be effective, and the thresholds are enormous.

\section{The formalisation}\label{sec:lean}

Appendix~\ref{app:claims} names the Lean theorems that prove each numbered result of Sections~\ref{sec:intro}
to~\ref{sec:certificate-b}, and the results from the literature that the proofs use; they are in \file{lib/} of the
repository \cite{repo} or in Mathlib. \file{formalisations/} documents the results from the literature and the further
classical results behind them. The Lean statements of Theorems~\ref{thm:2110} and~\ref{thm:4320} are
{\small
\begin{verbatim}
theorem linnik_21000 : ∃ K : ℝ, ∀ (q : ℕ) [NeZero q] (a : ℕ),
    a.Coprime q → ∃ p : ℕ, p.Prime ∧ p ≡ a [MOD q] ∧
      (p : ℝ) ≤ K * (q : ℝ) ^ (2100 / 1000 : ℝ)

theorem linnik_21500 : ∃ K : ℝ, ∀ (q : ℕ) [NeZero q] (a : ℕ),
    a.Coprime q → ∃ p : ℕ, p.Prime ∧ p ≡ a [MOD q] ∧
      (p : ℝ) ≤ K * (q : ℝ) ^ (2150 / 1000 : ℝ)
\end{verbatim}
}
where \lean{[NeZero q]} says $q \ge 1$, the power is the real power, $2100/1000 = 21/10$ and $2150/1000 = 43/20$. The
file \file{lib/check/Check.lean}, the repository's default build, prints the axioms of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} and of
the library's theorems that Appendix~\ref{app:claims} names for Sections~\ref{sec:inputs} to~\ref{sec:proof};
\file{lib/check/CheckAll.lean} prints those of Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} and of the theorems named for
Sections~\ref{sec:window} to~\ref{sec:certificate-b}. \file{scripts/check\_axioms.py} checks that they are
\lean{propext}, \lean{Classical.choice} and \lean{Quot.sound} only. The axioms of a theorem cover its whole dependency
cone, so a \lean{sorry} anywhere in it would appear as \lean{sorryAx}; the library's own sources contain no
\lean{sorry}, no declared axiom and no \lean{native\_decide} (\file{scripts/check\_hygiene.py}).

The certificates of Propositions~\ref{prop:certificate} and~\ref{prop:certificate-b} are checked by kernel evaluation,
\lean{decide +kernel}, and not by compiled code, on definitions that are arithmetic on Lean's core natural numbers,
lists and booleans. At $43/20$ the search that the certificate's hints lead is evaluated in $1552$ parts, in $17$ files
of $143$ to $271$ seconds each, about $63$ minutes of the kernel's time, and the $256$ Buchstab
steps that join the parts are proved one by one. At $21/10$ it is evaluated in $107374$ parts, in $1150$ files, and
$29349$ Buchstab steps join the parts; the generator of the files predicts about $76$ hours of the kernel's time on one
core for them, so the default build does not include them, and the repository's continuous integration builds them
in stages. At the time of writing that build has not finished, so the kernel has not yet checked the certificate
at $21/10$ as a whole (\S S5); Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} is a theorem of the library once the build at the commit of
this paper ends without error.

The two theorems share Sections~\ref{sec:inputs} to~\ref{sec:decomp} but not their tests. The tests of
Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} are those of Definition~\ref{def:tests}, with the leaves' condition $(\mathrm L)$ alone
(the namespace \lean{SieveAd.Plain}); those of Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} accept the leaves cut in three as well
(Section~\ref{sec:leaves-three}), and their soundness uses the second density curve. So the import cone of
Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} contains no module of the library's formalisation of Chen, Gupta and Li's paper, and no
declaration of Ingham's estimate is reachable from it, by a static reachability over the identifiers of each proof
(\file{scripts/decl\_reach.py}), which over-approximates the constants used when, as here, the modules concerned
declare no simp lemmas, instances or attributes; Ingham's zero detector is used, in the proof of Huxley's estimate. The
toolchain is Lean \texttt{v4.35.0-rc3} with Mathlib at commit \texttt{0f64d30a}, and the repository's continuous
integration builds the library and checks the axioms on every change to it.

Among the statements that are not Lean theorems are: the historical statements of Sections~\ref{sec:intro}
and~\ref{sec:comparison}, among them the remarks on Naslund's, R.~Li's and Banks and Shparlinski's results; the
heuristic remarks of Sections~\ref{sec:intro}, \ref{sec:type2}, \ref{sec:type1}, \ref{sec:window}
and~\ref{sec:rules}, among them what the region needs of the kernel bound and of a density estimate and why the
leaves' region needs $g^*$, and the effectivity remark of Section~\ref{sec:proof}; the remarks that Chen, Gupta and
Li's Theorem~1.2 and Corollary~1.4 give $7/3 + \varepsilon$; the figures computed by the repository's scripts (the
shape of the region at $43/20$ and of the leaves' region at $21/10$, the statistics of the searches and of the
certificates, and the computations of Section~\ref{sec:comparison}); and what is said about the sources of the inputs
and their
independent checking.

Independent checking of the inputs is limited. OpenAI's own Lean formalisation of the main preprint's result
\cite{openai-lean} is OpenAI's record, not an independent one, and this library's proof of
Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} is the author's new formalisation of the companion preprint's route. Chen, Gupta and Li's
preprint \cite{cgl} has not been refereed; the library's proof of Input~\ref{inp:cgl} follows their route, with the
one-modulus case of Heath-Brown's large values estimate for
double sums \cite{hb79b}, which they quote, proved along Kerr \cite{kerr} and Jutila \cite{jutila}. Ingham's and
Huxley's estimates are refereed and classical; the library's proofs of them, by the large values of Dirichlet
polynomials and the fourth moment, are the author's. \S S5 gives the details: the Lean statements, the library's
proofs of the inputs, what the kernel checks, and the artefact.

\section{What the inputs give without the sieve, and the method's limits}\label{sec:comparison}

This section claims nothing new. It places Theorems~\ref{thm:2110} and~\ref{thm:4320} against what the same inputs
give by the classical argument, which has no sieve, and against the exponents known for special moduli; the table of
the introduction sums it up.

\subsection*{The classical deduction}

Suppose that for some $A \ge 2$ there is a uniform density estimate
\[
  N^*(\sigma, T; q) \le C (qT)^{A(1 - \sigma)} (\log qT)^B \qquad (1/2 \le \sigma \le 1,\ T \ge 2).
\]
With Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} it gives Linnik's theorem with every exponent $L > A$. The library's proof writes the
smoothed sum $\sum_{n \equiv a} \Lambda(n) e^{-n/x}$ through the characters, bounds each non-trivial character's sum by
a contour shift to the edge of its largest zero-free rectangle, at one of the levels $\theta_j = \min(1/2 + j\delta/2,
11/12)$, for a small $\delta > 0$ fixed in terms of $L - A$, and counts the characters at each level by the density
estimate at the height $T + 1$, with $T = 2 \log x + 2$: the characters with zeros of real part about $\sigma$
contribute about $(q^A/x)^{1 - \sigma}$ together, and none has a zero of real part above $11/12$. With it the library
proves:
\begin{itemize}
\item $L = 12 + \varepsilon$ from Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} alone: at most $\varphi(q) \le qT$ characters are
  counted for every $\sigma$, and at most one above $11/12$, which is the estimate with $A = 12$;
\item $L = 36/13 + \varepsilon$ with Ingham's estimate, whose exponent $3/(2 - \sigma)$ is $36/13$ at
  $\sigma = 11/12$;
\item $L = 12/5 + \varepsilon$ with Huxley's estimate (with Ingham's below $\sigma = 3/4$), which bounds the density
  exponent by $12/5$ on the whole range;
\item $L = 11667/5000 + \varepsilon = 2.3334 + \varepsilon$ with the curve $g^*$ of Proposition~\ref{prop:curve-star},
  whose pieces between $5/7$ and $131/168$ come from Input~\ref{inp:cgl}: its exponent is at most $11667/5000$ on the
  whole range.
\end{itemize}
Appendix~\ref{app:claims} names the Lean theorem of each. With Chen, Gupta and Li's density estimate the exponent is
$7/3 + \varepsilon$, by the same deduction from their Theorem~1.2 \cite{cgl} (exponent $A = 7/3$), which the library
does not formalise as they state it; the last item is what the library proves in its place, and it differs from $7/3$
by the rounding of the curve's steps. They do not state a Linnik exponent, but their Corollary~1.4 \cite{cgl}, on primes
in progressions in short intervals under a zero-free region, contains this one: for $h \le x$ it gives
$\psi(x + h; q, a) - \psi(x; q, a) \sim h/\varphi(q)$ whenever
$h/\varphi(q) \ge x^{1/2 + \varepsilon} q^{1/6} + x^{17/30 + \varepsilon}$, and with $h = x$ and
Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} as the zero-free region this holds at $x = q^L$ for every $L > 7/3$ and all large $q$.

\subsection*{Special moduli}

For special moduli exponents smaller than that of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} are known; that of
Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} is smaller than they are. Harman \cite{harman05, harman08} applied his sieve to primes in a
progression to one large modulus $q$, for Alford, Granville and Pomerance's construction of Carmichael numbers
\cite{agp}, and obtained $\pi(x; q, a) \gg x/(\varphi(q) \log x)$ for $q \le x^{0.4736}$ outside exceptional moduli,
where the density estimate gives $q \le x^{5/12}$. For the powers of a fixed prime, Banks and Shparlinski
\cite{banks-shparlinski} have $2.1115 = 1/0.4736$ (the value as cited in \cite[footnote~1]{cgl}), from Harman's range
and their zero-free region \cite{baier-pujahari}. R.~Li \cite{li-harman} adapts
his Harman-sieve calculation to primes in progressions to large moduli $q$ whose prime factors are all below $q^C$, for
an absolute constant $C > 0$ whose existence his theorem asserts without giving it, when the $L$-functions of the
primitive characters to the divisors of $q$ have no zero with $\operatorname{Re} s > 1 - (\log q)^{-3/4}$ and
$|\operatorname{Im} s| \le \exp(\varepsilon (\log q)^{3/4})$, with the $\varepsilon > 0$ of his Theorem~9; for the
powers of a fixed prime he deduces the exponent $1/0.4752 < 2.1044$ (his Theorem~11), improving Harman's range and
with it Banks and Shparlinski's exponent.
He presents these theorems as modifications of that calculation, without separate proofs. Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}
implies the zero-free hypothesis of his Theorem~9 for every large $q$, so if that theorem holds as stated, it gives with
Input~\ref{inp:half-plane} the exponent $1/0.4752 = 2.1043\ldots$ for every large modulus whose prime factors are all
below $q^C$, between the exponents of the two theorems.

\subsection*{What the half-plane is used for}

The classical deduction does not need the value $11/12$ of Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}: it works with any zero-free
half-plane $\operatorname{Re} s > 1 - \eta_0$, for a fixed $\eta_0 > 0$. The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} uses the
half-plane for the prime sums of Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-sum}, whose contour runs to its edge, for the top bin of
Section~\ref{sec:bins}, for the kernel bound, where the Pólya--Vinogradov inequality would do
(Section~\ref{sec:region}), and for the prime number theorem. With a narrower half-plane the bins would run up to $1 -
\eta_0$, and the zone condition for two variables of length $q$ at the top bin would need $\rho \ge 1/(25000\eta_0)$
in place of $1/2000$; the region, and so the certificate, would change, and this is not pursued. The proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:2110} uses the half-plane in the same places, and in the bins of its two further estimates: in the
estimate in one variable the sizes of the rest's prime sums run up to the top bin, so that no prime sum of length $Q$
exceeds about $Q^{11/12}$, and the classes that no count makes small are bounded by that alone
(Section~\ref{sec:window-estimate}). What the proofs use is that there are no zeros near $\operatorname{Re} s = 1$, and
in particular no exceptional zero.

\subsection*{Where the gain comes from, and its limits}

The deduction needs the zeros of every real part to contribute less than the main term, so its exponent is the
largest density exponent on $[1/2, 11/12]$, which is $12/5$ with Huxley's estimate, at $\sigma = 3/4$, and $7/3$ with
Chen, Gupta and Li's, at $\sigma = 5/7$. The sieve of Theorem~\ref{thm:4320} needs the zeros only in sums of two
variables near $q$ times a product of primes, where Theorem~\ref{thm:type2} holds for every $L$ and uses the density
estimate only above $0.7789$, and it recovers the primes from those sums at the cost that its certificate bounds. In
exponents of $x$ the
region shrinks as $L$ grows smaller, and more of the sieve's terms fall outside it.

With the rules of Sections~\ref{sec:type2} to~\ref{sec:certificate} alone the cost is $0.94847$ at $43/20$. On twenty
of the first prime's cells, about a quarter of that cost is the cells' width; each $0.005$ of exponent costs between
$5\%$ and $9\%$ of it; and the rules that this certificate lacks, the reversal of the terms of the sign $+$ among them,
are worth a few per cent each in the exact search (\S S6).

The rules of Sections~\ref{sec:window} and~\ref{sec:rules} are of another kind. The estimate in one variable evaluates
terms that are outside the region of two variables, and where, for each prime sum of its rest, a power of its length
is near $q$, it takes nothing from the density estimate. The leaves cut in three recover comparisons that the bilinear
Type~I estimate does
not reach. With them, the reversal of the terms of the sign $+$, the test at the two corners, cells of side $0.003$
and a plan that tries every configuration over the frozen parts, the search passes at $21/10$ with the cost
$0.97717$, on a certificate of about twenty-six and a half times the steps of that at $43/20$. So $0.023$ of the primes
is left:
$21/10$ is close to what these rules give as the search uses them. Going much further needs
Type~I information on longer products, or Type~II estimates on wider regions, which a better density estimate above
$0.7789$ would give only a little of (Section~\ref{sec:region}).

 & \lean{LinnikCGLSteps.charDensity\_inT} \\
Huxley's density estimate (\file{formalisations/}) & \lean{LinnikHuxStep.charDensity\_gen} \\
\quad its steps on $[131/168, 5/6]$ & \lean{LinnikStairs7.charDensity\_hx7\_5}, \\
 & \dots, \lean{LinnikStairs7.charDensity\_hx7\_19} \\
\quad from $5/6$ on & \lean{LinnikStep.charDensity\_step} \\
The Hal\'asz--Montgomery inequality (\file{formalisations/}), in the weighted forms that &
\lean{SieveTypeIIHM.hm\_duality\_wt}, \\
\quad the proof uses, here and in Huxley's estimate & \lean{LinnikReflect.hm\_duality\_W} \\
Mean values over the characters mod $q$ \cite{montgomery-vaughan, gallagher67, gallagher70} &
\lean{LinnikHybrid.mean\_value\_chars}, \\
 & \lean{LinnikHybrid.large\_values\_chars} \\
The hybrid fourth moment \cite{montgomery-topics}, which Bui and Heath-Brown state with an integral \cite{bui-hb};
here at one point for each character, with the bound $C(q(T + 2))^{1 + \varepsilon}$ &
\lean{LinnikFourth.fourth\_moment} \\
Behind Input~\ref{inp:half-plane}: Kubota's cubic theta function \cite{kubota, patterson, dunn-radziwill} &
  \lean{Eis.kubotaTheta} \\
\quad the quadratic large sieve over $\mathbb Z[\omega]$ \cite{hb-quadratic, goldmakher-louvel} &
\lean{Eis.quadLargeSieve} \\
\quad cubic reciprocity \cite{ireland-rosen} & \lean{Eis.cub\_recip} \\
\midrule
The classical deduction (Section~\ref{sec:comparison}) & \lean{Linnik.linnik\_of\_charDensity} \\
Its exponents $12 + \varepsilon$, $36/13 + \varepsilon$, $12/5 + \varepsilon$ and $11667/5000 + \varepsilon$ &
\lean{Linnik.linnik\_twelve}, \\
 & \lean{LinnikIngham.linnik\_thirtysix\_thirteenths}, \\
 & \lean{LinnikTwelve.linnik\_twelve\_fifths}, \\
 & \lean{LinnikG7.linnik\_g7d} \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
}
